This so frequently happens during the London dict of " Found drowned" was returned. season that none can doubt that there is ample room for a similar league as was recently started by several influential women in Paris, with a view to strictly avoid all establishments where the shop keepers are unkind to their workpeople. It requires a strong effort to take an interest in airy fairy summer frocks, when the sight of such creations causes one to shiver. But we must make the effort and trust meanwhile that the glorious late spring, and hot summer predicted by weather prophets, may really fall to our share. Nun's veiling is being used by the furlong by leading dressmakers, for early summer frocks, and this fabric possesses many advantages. It lends itself well to the rashionable accordian pleated, or gathered skirt, drapes, bangs, and wears excellently, and last but •by no means least is quite inexpensive. In muslin's, delaines, grass lawns, organdies and voiles, there is little fresh to record, but the new linens allow great 'strides from an artistic point of vipw. The shot varie- ties are exceedingly beautiful and these, elaborately strapped with silk, form some of the most dressy after- noon toilettes designed for summer wear. At the moment, homespuns of coarse weaving and in primitive colors, are the favourite wear. Such gowns, or coats and skirts, are just what we require during this uncertain month,and for travelling, or the holidays later on, they are most useful. Irish, Scotch, Welsh, home- spun., all are being patronised by tailors and dress- makers, and for hard wear, and excellent effect, they will prove difficult to beat. That useful little garment the coffee coat promises to shortly become much too popular to please the well- dressed woman. This is a matter forvegret, as for wear over a simple blouse at theatres, or restaurant dinners, or for home wear over a middle aged gown, this little garment is invaluable. And though effective it need not be by any means expensive. Quite short remnants of silk, lace, and ribbon, and a little patience, are all that are needed to turn out a dainty coffee coat. MADGE.

Ragged School and Mission, A Service of Song, entitled "Jessica's first prayer," "wag rendered in the above mission on Sunday after- noon by the choir of Sharp-street Mission, the connec- tive readings being given by Mr. J. W. Bouchier, of Stockton Heath. The vocal parts were well sung under the direction of Mr. G. H. Jones. During the service Miss Bouchier contributed two violin solos. In the evening a special service was held, the preacher being Mr. Gray, when selected music was given by the children and choir.

FASHIONS OF TO-DAY.

The Examiner, Friday, May 8, 1903. THE NIGHT OF THE CRIME.

Those who have to study economy in dress should treat their possessions with care, and they would soon find that they were well repaid for the extra trouble entailed by the smart appearance retained by their clothe generally. Indeed, mothers are far toe neglectful on this point in the bringing up of their daughters, and do not teach them to put everything that belongs to them -in its own proper place as neatly and sm9othly as when it was new—a habit which, when once acquired. lasts a lifetime and is invalu- able. Women do not seem at all to grasp the fact that most of 'their belongings are lying-idle more than they are worn, or they

A PRETTY TOQUE

would, I am sure, be more attentive to these little niceties, which are little things is themselves, but go so far towards a trimness of appearance. A girl who knows heiw to make her clothes wear well when she conies in from a walk does not hang her coat up by the loop which is generally placed inside the collar, but uses a coat-hanger, which keeps the garment in proper shape. The pretty toque sketched is one of those delicate affairs which do require careful treatment, being made of pleated creamy tulle with a cluster of blush roses and their Leaves resting on the hair at the back. The shape is a fashionable one, and a dressy toque of this style will make an otherwise plain toilette quite smart and suited ,to many social functions.

To keep a blouse well in shape it should always be hung up by the loops which are generally attached to the armholes, while the more elaborate ones for afternoon wear should be stuffed with tissue paper and laid carefully in a drawer. Gloves, likewise., demand their share of careful attention if they are expected to wear well, and one should never forget, when removing them, to turn them wrong side out, afterwards pulling each finger back again into shape and folding the

Local Ocldfellowship. • The monthly -meeting of the Grand Lodge 'of the Independent Order of Oddfellows (Manchester Unity) was held at the Pelican Hotel, the President, P.G. T. Healing in the chair. .After the usual business had been transacted an interesting lecture was given by Past-Grand Smithson of the "Constitution" lodge entitled "A tour through the High and Lowlands of &otlend." It was illustrated by limelight views, showing scenes of ancient castles and other places of great interest. The Lecturer quoted appropriate verses from the poems of Burns and Scott. There was a fair attendance of brethren, including D.G.M. Halford, P.P.G.M. J. Massey, P.P.G.3f. S. Baldry, P.G. W. Holbrook, P.G. J. Gartland, P.G. 0. Higham, L.G.M. P. Lester. A hearty vote of thanks was accorded to P.G. Smithson.

The St. Helens Anti-Co-operative Agitation. The St. Helens people hear very little now-a-days of the "great co-operative fight," but outsiders, with other and, perhaps, personal ends to serve, like to occasionally stir matters up. Locally the position is very well defined. The traders are satisfied that they have reduced the number of members of the Co-opera- tive Society, and the grocers have certainly benefited; but during the weeks on which the Co-operative Society pay quizterly " divi " the drapers and ready- made clothing establishments now miss a lot of customers. The lady co-operators formerly spent their " divi " at the large drapery establishments, but now it all goes to the stores. The " divi " is paid out of the office, and the members then simply go into the various departments and spend it. In this way the stores have had some tremendous weeks, and sales have gone up. On the other hand, a large number of people, who were really connected with the private traders and had identical interests, and who formerly traded at the stores, have seen the error of their way, and in this way the traders benefit. The Trades' Defence Association continues to work quietly and systematically by way of -circulars, &c.

The Resignation of Mr. E. Braide. There was an interesting gathering at the Patten Arms Hotel on Saturday afternoon, when Mr. E. Braide, whose retirement after 29 years service a dis- trict goods manager for the London and North- Western Railway Company was announced in last week's "Examiner," was the recipient of a handsome present from the staff at Warrington. Representatives were present from many neighbouring towns. The presentation consisted of a handsome silver tea and coffee service, with tray, and a pretty .silver rose bowl for Mrs. Braide. The tray bore the following inscrip- tion :—" Presented to Edward Braide, Esq., by the staff of the goods department, Warrington District, London and North-Western Railway, as a token of their esteem on his retirement from active service after having /sad the management of the district from March Ist, 1874, to April 30th, 1903."— Ur. Smith (Leigh and Bedford) presided and eulogistic speeches were delivered bk Mr. W. Woodall (Black- burn). Mr. Beesley (Blackburn), Mr. Fortune and Mr. Driver.--In reply Mr. Braide said he could not say that anyone on the North-Western bad ever had a better staff than he had. (Hear, hear.) Every agent had been up to the mark, and they had credit all over the line for doing their work in a most creditable manner. He thanked them deeply for the able assist- ance they had given him throughout his career. The company had always behaved to him in a !nest gener- ous manner. They had been most considerate to him, and in addition to the amount received as superannua- tion they had given him a nice round sum, retaining his servicps if required. (Loud applause.) Mr. J. T. Williams, who succeeds Mr. Braide as goods manager, also spoke and said he appreciated the honour of being selected as successor to such a gentleman, and if he wanted any advice or assistance he -would get it from Mr. Braide.—Last week Mr. I. T. Williams was presented at Abergavenny with a mag- miff cent dining room chime, gong and bell,cloe with bronzes to match, and a handsome chest of plate, together with a silver lamp, cake dish and other articles of plate ,for Mrs. Williams. The elock bore the inscri }mon :—" Presented to Mr. I. T. Williams, together with a chest of plate, by his colleagues and friends in the South Wales and the West of England distri ct of the Landon and North-Western Railway, as a token of esteem upon his appointment as manager of the Warrington district. Alergavenny, April, 1903."

A DAINTY GOWN.

glove into as near as possible to the shape it was when first purchased. In regard to foot- wear, never on any account wear the same pair of shoes day after day, as shoes, like their wearers, demand a certain amount of rest. If possible, slip a pair of trees into the ;hoes whenever they are taken off, as this keeps them in shape. If one cannot afford trees (although the price of these is now greatly reducedj, a good substitute is found in a newspaper stuffed tightly into the shoe: It will also help t 72, prnlrtng the existence of one's boots or shoes if time is taken by the Forelock and they are selected a few months before they will be brought into active work, as in this way the .3titches are allowed to set and the rawness to pass off. A dainty gown forms the subject of the iecond illustration. • It is made of pastel-pink cashmere, and has a deep collar of fine spotted muslin finished with lace. An appliqué of white silk gives a finish to the front of the gown and the loose upper sleeves, the under ileeves being of line net .to match the collar, caught in at the wrists by a band of soft 3ilk. This gown would look well lined with :ream-coloured silk, or if that is too expen- 3ive, sateen either contrasting or matching the colour of the cashmere. This gown is at Duce simple and elegant, an: would dc -ually

A CHARMING HAT/

well as a morning or tea gown. It is loose, and yet the outline of the figure is slightly defined at the sides in a becoming manner. If for afternoon wear, its 'dressiness could be emphasised by the addition to the front just where the points of the collar fasten of a bow, and long ends of soft silk or chiffon, while lace treated in the same way would also look- attractive. Every day the average woman seems to more and more realise the cosiness of a gown of this description, which is easily slipped on and off, and always looks and feels comfortable. Of course, one could evolve it into a much more elaborate affait if silk or satin were substituted for cashmere, or fine muslin interspersed with lace inser- tions; but it is not everyone who would find such dainty garments suited to their require- ments and surroundings. Very well suited to the young face of a girl of twelve to fourteen years of age is the charming hat depicted. It is made of fine green straw, with 'light folds of lace softening the curves of the brim. The sole trimming is a large bow of soft white silk ribbon spotted with black. The hat is really quite simple, and its brim can be curved and bent to suit the face of the wearer. There are many ornamental and lovely hats composed ol silk and lace, or lace entirely, which will be well worn later on in the season.

There are very many fanciful straws this season, which are so lacy in design as to form the trimming for many smart samples oi millinery. These are twisted into rosetteE or bows, and look very smart. Some of dm best models depend on their shape alone fin their smartness, as there is little or no trim- ming on them. Picture hats, however, con- tinue to be adorned with Wreaths of flowers and foliage, but it is noticeable that the flowers are all of the graceful, droopinf 'character, such as the fuschla or convolvulus, whose soft tones of colour harmonise with any scheme of dressi CoQuEnio

BY THE

MAJESTY driving' with PRESIDE ihrough OP famous ARC de Tin

PARIS, Sunday.'

Paris is at last quiet. A thousand pens are 'busy depicting the surpassing beauty of the night's illuminations, the enthusiastic scenes and universal festivity that greeted " Edouard" as he drove like a prince through the fairyland of the boulevards to the gorgeous spectacle awaiting him without and within the opera hduse. Paris is wrapped in repose under the fast paling stars, and surrounded by the lingering glow of myriads of electric stais Still holdirvir forth for tb, delight of those

nig OPtAt. mOusE. IMAM sleepless vestiges of the populace that haunt the boulevards. the Rue Royale, and the neighbourhood of the Place Vendome. Paris, worn out with her two days and nights of rejoicing, dancing, feasting, and general jubi- lation over the advent of a king worthy of her embrace, is sleeping softly in the fragrant dawn. The little milliners of the Rue de la Paix have vanished to their attics to dream of the " Edouard," the good king of their gene- rous customers " l'Anglais " on whose confec- tions they have laboured for many weary days and nights. For were they not per- rnitted a sight of " Edouard" as he drove by in the golden light of a perfect day, or surrounded by the brave " soldats" on their Splendid steeds, guarding him as he swept past under the brilliant arches of electric lights on return from the opera? Vive le Roi ! Vive Edouard ! The mighty roar still echoes from boulevard to rue. From the towers of Notre Dame, where the flags .Woke to the fluttering breeze of a perfect 'clawn, to the glimmering lights still hovering t(Aind the historic Tour St. Jacques. From the silent fields of Vincennes, where the king witnessed the military glory of France, to the green swards of. Longchamps, where the beauty and flower of Parisian society assem- bled to grret him and behold "John Bull" come in first winner the echoes of that mighty ovation still live. Never has London equalled the splendour Of this magnificent reception of Paris to her favourite "Le Prince" of the old days, whom She affectionately welcomes now as le Roi Edouard." The Coronation and Queen's Jubilees rolled into one would not surpass the glory of this long day of festivity in honour of the King, for verily it has been one continuous clay of rejoicing—since the night has been vanquished by the brilliancy of a million Miniature suns of electricity flashing from the tower Eiffel to the tour St. Jacques, and em- bracing in its dazzling chain the whole of this great city of pleasure from the river Seine to the heights of Montmartre this festival of light, colour and decoration is carried out on the poetic lines of some tourney of the days 01 "cloth of gold." courtyard. To the left of the Embassy, some hundred feet distant, begins the grounds of the presidential residence, the Elysees Palace. The massive wall enclosing the palace bears at suitable intervals trophies of the Tricolour with shields bearing the Republican cipher— R. F.—and various mottos adopted by the Republic. The King's visit to the Elysees Palace to make official greeting to the Presi- dent, and the unofficial visit to the Theatre Francaise in the evening prepared Paris for the festivities which culminated last night. The morning of Saturday broke with a joyous promise of fine weather: a promise fully redeemed by weather of the most magni- ficent description, the one thing needful to fill the Parisiennes' cup of pleasure to the brim. The whole town was astir early—all up with the market waggons and streaming to the various points of egress to the road Vincennes. The Metro, the trains, the omnibusses, all were crowded to the utmost in the eagerness of the crowd to reach the grounds of Vincennes in good time for the review. The tooting of automobile horns made the air resound from six o'clock onward. It seemed impossible that the crowd would find standing room, but they did. Vincennes is a series of vast fields sur-

rounded by a fringe of trees and many miles in circumference. Frantic cheers greeted the King as he descended .from the carriage and took his place with President Loubet in the front of the royal stand. A group of officers, ambassadors and French officials surrounded His Majesty. It was truly an imposing scene, the vast field giving ample room for the manoeuvres oi the troops. The sun shone in full regal splendour on the gay course at Longchamps when the King and President arrived to view the races. His Majesty had donned the conventional frock coat, and wore his favourite flower in the button-hole. The fatigue of the morning review and his reception at the Hotel de Ville, with its famous toasts, had passed away under the genial influence of the cic'jettner at the Em- bassy, where his Majesty entertained a host of old friends and officials, and I have never seen him looking better than when he appeared in the royal box. Madame Loubet, attended by her daughters, occupied the place of honour pour les dames. The King was visibly moved by the warmth of his reception, and applauded

aeartily when Chrysothemis, a scion of his majesty's Derby winner Persimmon, won the first race. A view of the illuminations and the King's progress to the opera closed a memor- able and glorious day. To the left in a sky of purple luminous space the young moon hung her silver crescent over the dark mys- terious outline of the Madeleine. Far below a sparkling mast arose like an irridescent vapout trom the illuminations of the Rue Royale.

A POPULAR COMEDIENNE. Discusses Stage Perils.

Mks Macho Harcourt the popular music-hall favourite, hss been talking. to a London paper about Stage Perils. Staxs "of the theatrical world are envied for their big salaries : but (says Miss Harcourt) " people little know the struggles which their favourite artistes have to go through." One danger Ivhich Miss Harcourt knows how to avert was described by her in a chat in her charming „house ats, St. Mary's Street_,_ Kennington Road, Londor

this anecdote, a I contracted, while attending a Carden Party,* chill which led •to rhetimatic fever. But for Dr. IVilliatuss pink pills for pale people that Garden Party would have cost me dear ! I was laid up for a fortnight, and when I recommenced my Professional Engagements I felt all to pieces.' My nerves were completely shattered. I suffered with -terrible head- achesand sickness, and it was often a fearful ordeal for me to go through my turn.' All food seemed nause- ous, and my appmite became so poor that my weight was reduced in two months two stone and a half. I used to lie in bed half the day,' •und then crawl out to the theatre to do my turn.' I seemed to have lost all my energy, twid my complexion went ae yellow•as saffron. T actually paid a boy to see me safelvto the stage door, T felt so ill. I consulted doctors in several towns, but it was not until after •I had tried Dr. Williams* pink pills that I got any relief at all. The first box I took did me a lot of good, and T. improyed with every„dose I took, until now, after taking three boxes, I can safely say lam thoroughly cured. I feel as tit as a fiddle,' and can eat like a Trojan. It is a 'mystery to me how all the horrible headaches, biliousness, languor, and giddinness have been so quickly and • completely ban- ished ; but I am glfid enough ty know that they have gone from me

, r SNUTHPORT'S GAS PR.OFITS.—It is anticipated that the Southport gas statement will show a profit of £lO,OOO for the past year's working. The Warrington Town Gmncil at its meating on Tuesday further distussed the motion which was intro- duced at the last Council meeting byCouncillor Dodds. The motion read as follows :--` That inasmuoh as the duties in connection with the Technical Institute for which Mr. Charles Madeley has been receiving an addi- tional £5O per year have been transferred to the new Director of Education, the salary of Mr. Madeley 'be forthwith 'reduced from £250 per 'annum, plus house, Sc., to £2OO per annum„ plus house, &c." - Alderman MONKS who had the right to speak first, said he did not wish to do so for the moment.

The M.avoit : Of course those who have already spoken will not have the right to speak again. Councillor BARTON said a great deal of time had been spent in speaking upon Mr. Madeley's qualifications, but ha thought as a matter of principle the money shouldbe reduced. Id the present state of trade they should try as far as possibleto meet thewishes of the townspeople. They should be as ()Ireful in spending public money as in spending their own. (Hear. hPar.) Councillor TINN EON thought there was need for further explanntion. He understood the Town Clerk to say that Mr. Madeley was drawing his salary by resolution of that Council, and therefore he failed to understand how they could be paying Mr. Madeley £5O more than he was entitled to. It was not a question whether Mr. Madeley was worth £'.250 but a question whether the thing had been done in a proper, straightforward, and legal manner. if the thing had not been done legally then the best way would be to accept the resolution proposed by Mr. Dodds, and afterwards proceed to give Mr. Madeley ah increase of 5.:50 perannum. Mr. Madeley was not only librarian but also curator of the Museum, and had something to do with the School of Art. Some of the speeches made in that Council might be taken as reflecting upon the services rendered by Mr. Madeley, and an endeavour had been made to show that he was receiving more than was paid in other towns. That was simply because in most other towns the services given were those of librarian only. He would like to knew whether the committee had any information as to what salwies were paid in other towns for similar duties to those discharged by Mr. Madeley. He did not think the movers of the motion objected to giving Mr. Madeley a fair remuneration,and he thought they were ready to do so.

Councillor DODDS : I did not give an opinion either way.

_ Councillor Ms-NEON: I should like to know whether the Councilhas sanctioned this £250?

The TOWN CLERK', reefying to Councillor Tinnion, said in December last the Museum Committee expressed their appreciation of Mr. Madeley's work, and strongly recommended that his .salary should not be diminished in consequence of the proposed change in his duties. , That recommendation was approved by the Council. ' Councillor TINNION :Ts that binding upon the Council? The TOWN CLERK: Undoubtedly, sir. Councillor JA.MES•II_IVANS slid he took it that Coun- cillor Dodds' motion was in order. He thought it would be a very proper thing now to give notice that the motion should be rescinded, as their Town Clerk now ruled that they were bound by the previous resolution. Councillor Dorms: I assume that lam in order. Who gays that I am not? _ _ _

_ The NIAYOR: lam advised th.it special notice is required. Councillor DODDS : I gave special notice. Counoillor JAMES EVANS said it was rather curious that when they came to certain difficulties they were found to be out of order. When they discussed the • question at the last Council meeting no exception was taken then on the ground that they were out of order, . but now that they were proceeding with it,it wt's inti - ; mated that it was necessary that they should give I special notice of motion rescinding the former resolu- tion.

The AAvoa suggested that if the Council was willing they might go on with the matter that night. Councillor DODDS : I hold that is the only thing the Council can do. CouncillorThsrox said he had a distinct recollection of the ruling of the Town Clerk, and he had thought it desirable that he should explain fairly to the open Council, and had brought it forward entirely upon his own initiative.

The MA YOR ; Do you mean there ought to be another notice?

Councillor TENsioN : No, I don't. I think there ought to be a personal explanation by the Town Clerk. That is why I raised the question. There had been no collusion as might be suggested by the remarks of Councillor Evans, and he denied and repudiated any such suggestion. They were open to all kinds of base inshuntions and it was about time some- one put his foot down. Councillor EVAN'S: I hope, Mr. Mayor, that you will allow me to say just one word. Alderman SUTTON (who rose at the same time) said he objected to any member speaking twice. The MA volt stated that Alderman Sutton was in order. Councillor Evans was out of order. -

Conncillor EVA.NS: I am not particular whether Alder- man Sutton'objects or does not object. I maintain that Councillor Tinnion has misrepresented everything Ilsaid upon the point. The MASOR. I. can only allow you to speak as a matter of personal explanation. Alderman SurroN: I think Councillor Evans has spoken so many times that he is out of order. Councillor Evass : Not so many times as Alderman Sutton. (Laughter). • Alderman SUTTON said some members•took up half an hour in saying what could easily be said in live minutes. (Laughter). He thought the proper course would be to give notice rescinding the former resolution before they further discussed the question. Alderman Momcs said the 9uestion raised two im- portant points and divided itself into two distinct issues. As far as he could see those who occasioned the discussion wished to set something right that was wrong. The movers of the motion before them claimed. that the recommendation that had been brought for- ward, and which everyone had had the opportunity of commenting upon, and which had received the unanim- ous approval of the Council, was not in order, and that that course of procedure was wrong, and that +$ because that motion was approved of, the Finance Committee and the Museum Committee were cut of order in con- tinuing to pay Mr. Madeley the salary he had hitherto been receiving. If their object was to set something right which was wrong,the proper way was to rescind the resolution referred to which they claimed was out of order. They.had not done anything of the sort. He could only conclude that they were anxious 'to give Mr. Madeley a kick in the back in an indirect sort of way without it being too apparent on the surface. If the supporters of the resolution were anxious to set things right and not to give Mr. Madeley a reduction, he (Alderman Monks) was prepared to accept the reso- lution on the understanding that Mr. Madeley's salary was not interfered with. If that was not agreed to, the resolution was an attempt to reduce a man's salary and therefore he would have to go into the further question of the reasons which induced the Museum Committee to take the course they did.He did notknow whether Mr. Dodds had anything to say on thia point. If he had it might shorten the discussion. Councillor DODDS said he had been unable to hear all that Alderman Monks had said. He understood that a pistol was being WO to his head. Alderman MONKS said he had no notion of holding a pistol .to Councillor Dodds' head. That was not a proper light in which to discuss the matter. If the object was to set something right and not to do a seri- ous injustice to a public' servant he was prepared to accept Councillor Dodds' motion on condition that Mr. Madeley's salary would not be interfered with, because the committee felt that Mr. Madeley was worthy of the salary and fully entitled to the RUM.

Councillor DOD6S said he ITvas-4-uft,;--p-repared to stand by the resolution. If members of the Council wanted to increase Mr. Madeley's salary in a proper and straightforward way they could do so. He was not going to undertake to bring it forward. Alderman Moncs : I did'nt ask you.

Councillor DODDS: You asked me to give an under- taking that another resolution would be brought for- ward. I will do nothing of the kind. Alderman MONIM said this was an attempt to reduce Mr. Madeley's salary in away which was very unfair. He would confine himself exclusively to the reasons that induced the Musettm Committee to make the recommendation. • Councillor Dom's: The Technical Sub-Committee.

Alderman MONKS pointed out that the Museum Com- mittee had approved the recommendation of the sub- committee, and had submitted the suggestion. to the Council as a recommendation, and the Council had approved. Therefore Councillor Dodds was wrong in saying it was the recommendation of the sub-committee,

.• Councillor DODDS : I was present at the meeting of the Musetun Committee, and the matter was not discussed in any way whatever.

• •, .•••"1,1-, • Va• Alderman Atoxics said the Museum Committee were so satisfied that they did not feel it was worth while discussing the matter. They felt that Mr. Madeley had justly earned this £5O per annum. In 1900 Mr. Made- ley asked for an advance of salary, and a special sub- committee was appointed to consider the matter. They also considered the question of other salaries, and made collectionvof various facts and figures as to what was paid in other towns. The sub-committee came to the conclusion that Mr. Madeley was fairly entitled to an

advance, but that in the event of possible changes upon the completion of the Technical School it might be necessary to reorganize the whole staff, and in view of those changes the Committee did not recommend an advance then and there, but thought that it was better to wait to see what reconstruction might be necessary. At the end of that time they had gone again into the matter, and they had decided that it was a proper thing to divide the appointments, and to appoint a director of education. As they knew, that had been done. The sub-committee felt, and the Museum Committee also felt, that the lengthy services that Mr. Madeley had rendered to the town, not only as librarian—and this was a point that inanypeople forgot or overlooked— but also as curator of the Museum and Art Gallery, deserved acknowledgment. As far as he knew there was no other town where one individual held these several offices. In many towns the librarian would also look after the art gallery. and many had curators for their museum only. Aha of salaries was given in "The Warrington Times" last week with the object of show- ing that Mr. Madeley was overpaid. Those figures' did not show that the figures were for duties as librarian only, an there was no fair comparison. It was neither fair to the Committee nor to Mr. Madeley. At Wigan, a town of 60,000 inhabitants, the librarian looked after the library only and was paid ,e3OO a year. Bury was a town of 58,000 inhabitants where the librarian was appointed in 1901 and paid £193. St. Helens, a town on somewhat similar lines to Warrington, paid £2OO to the librarian only. Ashton-underLyne, a similar place, with a small art gallery, paid .2200 a year. Proceeding he pointed out that a letter which the chairman of the Museum Committee had sent to every member stated that Mr. Madeley was first appointed in 1874 at a salary of £l4O and that there had been no increase since 1895. They had, taken that into account, and had gone also into the nature of the work because it was work of a special character that required special training, and Mr. Madeley had special 'qualifications which very few librarians had for doing this particular kind of work. It was very rare indeed that they found an individual who had an aptitude for combining the work of librarian with that of curator of a museum such as they had in Warrington. Mr. Madeley had acted as lo gid secretary for conducting the various examinations, otifTf which he had received certain fees. Last year he made £lB out of these, and that was of course lost to him owing to the fact that a new Director of Education had been appointed. In future this sum would be credited to the Education Committee and not to the Director of Education, so that that motion meant a total reduction to Mr. Madeley of £lB per year. The committee had no ulterior motive in making the recommendation in the form in which it appeared, and they had dor% nothing that was illegal or out of order and nothing that was contrary to the rules and regulations of the Council,and full opportunity had been given to every member to have his say. It was rather late in the day to object now that the Council had paid the salary. Mr. Madeley was a hard-working, earnest, conscientious, painstaking ser- vant of the Corporation,and was fullyand fairly entitled to his salary. The matter might seem to have been done in a nou'ative sort of way, but the committee had long felt that there was something due to him, and had only postponed the question of salary for the reasons that he •had stated. He hoped the Council would be satisfied with the explanation he had given. The MANOR said he understood that it had been sug- gested that this was a question of principle, apart from Mr. Madeley's deserts. Alderman Surrox said his suggestion was that they should pass the resolution and make the salary £2OO a year, and bring forward a resolution afterwards as to the advance.

Oonncillor DODDS: That is my main point. Alderman SUTTON: I suggest that to Alderman Monks.

Alderman MONKS: The difficulty is that the time for advancing salaries has gone by. Unless we suspend the Standing Orders it would not be possible to bring forward a resolution.

The TowN CLEttic You have power to suspend the Standing Orders.

Alderinan MONKS: That would get over the difficulty.

Alderman Surros : Yes, it would put us. out of a difficulty, and be the pleasantest course to adopt. Councillor DODDS' in replying on the discussion, said he was rather hurt by a remark made by Councillor Bennett at the last meeting of the Council, when he characterized the rilsolution as a very shabby one, and that he was very sorry to see Mr. Dodds' name attached to it. He had no objection to Councillor Bennett or any other member of the Council attacking him in regard to shabbiness or want of generosity in his private capacity, but in relation to public matters he could neither be shabby nor generous; he must be careful of the public money. If he might give a little advice to Councillor Bennett it would be that he should think a little more and talk a little less glibly, let his thoughts come before his words rather than his words before his thoughts. (Laughter.) He reminded him very lunch of the story John Bright used to tell of an occurrence in the House of Commons. Mr. Cobden,Mr.Villiers and Mr. Bright were sitting together and there was a gentleman on the Conservative benches who was very fluent and fond of gesticulating and alluding to him. One of the three,Mr. Cobden, said to Villiers and Bright "He makes a very good speech if you don't listen to what he says.' (Laughter.) He (Councillor Dodds) had brought the matter of Mr. Madeley's salary forward because a great many of the ratepayers outside had put it to him that the £5O advance in salary had not received the sanction of the Council, and nearly one-half of the members had admitted to him that they were not satisfied that it had been done in a regular and proper manner.He was not going to commit' himself one way or another as to the merits of Mr. Madeley. Fifteen months ago Mr. Madeley had an assistant curator appointed to help him at a salary of £BO or £9O per year. He thought his resolution was justified. He had been told by threemembers of the Museum Committee that they objected to the way the £5O had been given, and that Mr. Madeley himself was dissatisfied. Let the Council put it right. If Mr. Madeley was worth the extra salary then let it be voted in the proper way.

Aldeiman Mos said he wanted to put Mr. Dodds right. Mr. Dodds had said that an assistant mArator was appointed to assist Mr. Madeley in the work of the Museum. That appointment was merely to fills vacancy caused by the resignation of Mr. Lowe who had been appointed to fill a vacanc3 at Portsmouth where he was getting £250 a year. Angling. By the confirmation of the scheme for re-organiza- tion of the Warrington Anglers' Association, anglers can now by the payment of Os. per annum, fish the following waters, viz. : the Old River at Newbridge, the Old River at Lynun, • Acker's Pits at Latchford. Runcorn and Latchford Canal at Walton, the lower length of the river &Mu. at Heatley, and the whole of the Bridgewater Canal, the latter by arrangement with the Worseley Canal Fishery Board. Such mem- bers can also participate in all the contests of the Society, and also take part in the competition for specimen prizes. For the sum of Bs. 6d. per annum, anglers can fish in all the above waters held by the Association, and in addition the upper length of the river Bollin at Heatley and Dunham' Massey, and in any new water that may be acquired. To take part in all the contests and to "' weigh in" for specimen prizes is ale° included in the privileges. All members desirous of entering the higher class, are requested to notify the general secretary at their earliest convenience and to forward their membership card to be endorse:!. A scheme, having for its objeet the federation of all local angling clubs,is to be drafted by the secretaries of the Association and the chairman, secretary and treasurer of each local angling .club is to be summoned to a special meeting to be held at an early date to discuss-the matter and if possible to arrive at a conclusion whereby such an object can be accomplished. The General Wolfe A.S. .have fixed July 2rith and August 29th as the dates for their matches. The opening match of the Mersey A.S. will be fished on June 21st. A trout of 11b. 1 loz. was re- corded from the Bollin by Mr. Webstet before the heavy rains came last week.

Samuel Herbert Dougal appeared before thd Saffron Walden magistratas, on Wednesday, charged with the murder of Miss Holland, of the Moat Farm. The prin- cipal evidence was given by Dr.gepper, the Home Office expert, who said that the bullet wound in the head could not have been self-inflicted. The bullet found in the skull was similar to those found at the Moat Farm. Accused was again remanded.

He said ten letters daily went to the deitd letter. office through having no address; a thousand registered letters were returned to the returned letter office yearly, containing £600,000 or £700,000 in money. besides valuables; and in the course of a year 25,000,000 postal packets could not be de- livered through incorrectness or insufficiency of address.

MR. WALTER LONa THE SPEED OF made an announcement of „ MOTOR-CARS. some importance oa

Tuesday. He told the House of Commons, in reply to a question on the subject, that he has in preparation a Bill dealieg with the speed and the general legal status of motor-cars. The measure will, we have no datibt, abolish the present speed limit. There are few motorists who do not now and again, and when- ever a good opportuaity presents itself, infringe the existing law. Wnen he was the other day in- specting a new car which has been built for him in Paris, the KING made inquiries as to what was the top speed to be obtained, and he was told 32 miles an hour. Both question mid answer were significant. Those whopurchase motor-cars capable of a speed of thirty-two miles an hour, do Hot intend to uniformly travel at a rate of more than twelve miles. As a matter of fact the law is constantly being broken by motorists, many: of whom are themselves law makers. Mr. BALFOUR'S car, lent to a candidate in a recent Par- liamentary election, is reported to have at least once put en a speed of forty miles an hour. There is really no harm in this if the road is clear, if the driver of the car sees his way well ahead, ani if he can bring his vehicle to a standstill almost at once when occasion arises. Obviously, however, these high speeds must be attended by great danger to the public anywhere but in places where the roads are practically deserted. In the streets of towns they should, of course, be made impossible in theory and indeed in law. The pedestrian has just as much right to the use of any part of the thoronghf are as the driver of horses or of motor cars, but if he presumes on that right to any extent he does so distinctly at his peril. It appears to be a fixed idea among drivers of vehicles that the foot pas- senger should confine himself to the pavement. Refuges are becoming more necessary in the streets; and in the more populous towns, if the drivers of motor-cars have their way, underground passages at all the more important *crossings may become indispensable. Yet wedoubt whether the present speed on motor vehicles is the best mode of dealing with what is an increasing danger. The question put by Mr. J. Erias to the President of the Local Gavernmeet Board on Tuesday, and evidently suggested by considerations of public safety, took it for granted that the law would he placed on a more satisfactory footing if the limit as to speed were removed, subject, as Mr. Emig said, to due security for the safely and con- venience of other users of public roads by re- quiring registration of the vehicles, and by enacting a high maximum penalty for breaches of these and any other conditions it might seem desirable to impose. Mr. LONG will at present give no indication of what his Bill is to contain. Probably he does not himself know. What it will be necessary to guard against in such new legisla- tion as would be merely in the interests of the owners of motor-cars. The public safety must be the first consideration. It is much less important that motor-oar owners should be able to rush along at high speeds than that the lives of his MAJESTY'S subjects should be safe on the lirso's highway. It does not follow that a mere speed limit—which, as we know, is only, observed when s policeman is in sight— is the best means of secur- ing the desired end. Tne law as to furious driving might meet most cases, but the penalties, as Mr. Eraas suggests, should be so increased as to create a real sense of serious responsibility among thoo who appear to think that since motor-cars carne into use the roads and the street are their own.

Substantial advantages are offered vtt%'llich the Rural Council cannot give them. The terms, are moderate in the extreme, and it really means t for the next ten years the Runcorn taxpayer will Ps bearing, out of all proportion, the burden of direct irsprovements for Weston and Halton which he will no tea -11 Y participate in.

The discussion on the minutes of the Technical _lsstruction Committee at the meeting of the Runcorn Urban Council revealed the striking fact that the four scholarships offered by the local authority had all been carried off by boys from the National School. RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

SERIOUS FALL AT RUNCORN

On Saturday a young man named Henry Critchley, of Wolverhampton, had a terrible fall at, the Weston Point Salt Works of the Salt Union, Limited. Critchley, while employed in the construction of a motor-power gas plant, fell from a scaffold 30,feet high. He became jambed in the iron work eight feet from the floor, and was unconscious when extricated. His left shoulder is smashed, his body badly bruised, and he suffers severely from shock to the system. Dr. Delaney is attending the injured man, who still lies in a dangerous condition.

===== 0002601_19030508_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
THEFT OF MONEY BY A SEAMAN,

At the Runcorn Police Court, on Wednesday, Frederick Gascoign, seaman, was charged with having stolen Rijn money belonging to Henry Ammindsen, able seaman on the brigantine " Taragena."—From the evidence it transpired that the .prisoner, was formerly engaged on the brig but was discharged at Goole. Having nothing to do he followed the ship to Runcorn Docks. Prisoner there saw Ammindsen, who had a sovereign and a florin in a box underneath his pillow, and said he was going away out of the town. Subse- quently the sovereign was missed, and prisoner was found to have changed one at the Railway Hotel. He was arrested at Widnes. Prisoner was committed to gaol for three months with hard labour.

TRANSFERS OF LICMSES, "sci 9

At Runcorn Petty Sessions on Monday before Mijor Hazelhurst (in the chair), and Mr. U. L. Davies,Richard Kendrick Williams applied for the transfer of the licence of Wilson's Hotel, lately held by William Wildgoose. The application was granted.—Ganrge Carson was granted the transfer of the licence from Elizabeth Horton, of the Mersey Vaults, Mersey-street.—Susan Fitton, licensee of the Watertnan's Arms, Weston Point, applied for the house to be put in the name of Susan Langley, as she had recently been married. The name was accordingly altered.—George H. Andrews produced plans for the alteration of the "Elephant and Castle" beerhouse, Halton, of which he was the licensee. The plans were approved.

A SUNDAY NUISANCE. At Runcorn Petty Sessions, on Monday, Ross Sheep, aged 17, Herbert Yarwood, 15, John Kelly, 18, John Brown, 22, John Flannagan, 28, Thomas Faulkner, 27, Thomas Jones, 18, Joseph Morris, 18, Thomas Watson, 116, each pleaded guilty to playing " banker" on Sunday , the 26th April. P.C. Davies in proving the case stated ' that on the date mentioned he watched the defendants playing on the Ship Canal Bank, opposite Belvedere, -from 5.30 until 7. When he tried to get near them they ; ran away. He had since succeeded in getting theirnames. Superintendent Ennion said there had been many com- plaints about the defendants playing for money. Flannagan, who had been previously convicted, and was, apparently, the ringleader of the gang was fined RA including costs or fourteen days, and the others 10s. in- cluding costs or seven days.

COUNTY ASYLUM RATE.

Mr. A. Horaby, Clerk to the Committee of Visitors, County Asylum Chester, wrote informing the Board, that the Committee had fixed the rate of maintenance of pauper lunatics in the Asylum at 7s. 7d. per head per week, for the quarter ending the 30th June. The Chairman: Any alteration?

The Clerk: No! FRESH ACCOMMODATION REQUIRED FOR HARMLESS

LUNATICS.

The Mailing Union forwarded copy of a resolution passed by them with reference to the accommodation and cost of lunatics and expressed the hope that the Runcorn Guardians would support it. The Clerk said that a similar resolution had been passed at the Northern Poor Law Conference to which the Board sent delegates. They were now taking the. matter up. Mr. Norman said they ought to support it. He felt there should be a home where simple cases could be sent. He moved that the Board support the circular. Mr. Woodward in seconding, presumed that such an institution would deal with lunatics and epileptics of a mild type. It was painful for such to go to a lunatic asylum or to remain in a workhouse. England was behind the times in the matter.

The resolution was adopted. PA UPE ELS SAVINGS.

_ Mr. F. Stretton, clerk to the Isle of Wight Union wrote, enclosing a copy of resolution passed at the last meeting, asking the ituncorn Board to take similar action 'Resolved that a communication be addressed to the Controller of the Post Office Savings Bank De- partment, informing him that the Board have had to deal with cases where, upon the death of a recipient of relief, it has transpired that such a person had a con- siderable sum of money deposited in the Post Office Savings Bank and asking, in the event of the Board suspecting a similar state of affairs, whether the Con- troller would, on receipt of particulars give the Guardians sufficient information to enable them to deal with the case." _ . .

It was resolve-I that the resolution receive the support of the Board on the proposition of Messrs. Norman and Knowles.

RESIGNATION OF LADY BROOK E.

A letter dated May 4th was read by the Clerk.announe- ing that Lady Brooke had with much regret, determined to resign her position on the Ladies Visiting Committee as she was so seldom able to be present. . Mr. Wright proposed that Lady Brooke be asked to reconsider her decision.- She had been very good to the inmates and it would be a pity if she severed her con- nection with them. Mr. Norman selonderl and the motion was carried. TILE STATE OF THE HOITSE.

1.• Mr. Moseley Williams reported that the condition of the House was most mtisfactory. There were five less inmates than last year. Mr. Dansey, who had seen him that morning, had suggested several alterations to the proposed baths which would involve additional expendi- ture, The matter, however, could be • left over till the next meeting.

A petition is being prepared praying for the reprieve of Gunner Hudson, of the R.F.A., who was sentenced to de:tth at the last Manchester Assizes for the murder of his comrade, Bombadier Short, at Falwood Bar;acks. The petition is expected to benumerouslyand influenti- ally signed. The execution is fixed for Tuesday next. During a severe thunderstorm at Dawley, Shropshire, on Tuesday night, a schoolboy was killed by lightning and another was badly injured. A young man named Allard, of Bulinghatn, Worcestershire, and several of his, relatives sought refuge in a galvanized shed. The structure was struck by lightning, and Allard was killed. His'uncle was knocked down but uninjured, and hi:, sister was slightly hurt.

NEWTON URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL

Ordinary Monthly Mgating.

The ordinary monthly meeting of the Urban District Council of Newton-in-Makerfleld washeld at the Town Hall Earlestown on Monday evening, Mr. W. Coiling- wood presiding. The minutes of the whole of the Com- mittees held on the 20th of April were confirmed.

THE PROPOSED FREE LIBRARY.

Dr. W. Latham moved the adoption of the Public Libraries Act for the district and commented upon the need of a free library in the neighbourhood. He said ,that the Council could borrow money to build a library in the event of Mr. Carnegie refusing his help, and the repayment of this loan would be more than covered each year by a penny rate. A heated discussion ensued, Mr. Gilmour strenuously opposing the project, on the grounds that the Institute and Library in connection with the London and North Western Railway Wagon Works at Earlestown amply supplied the needs of the District. The motion was eventually rejected. LAMPLIGHTERS' GRIEVANCES.

At a meeting of the Gas Committee, it was reported that the Chairman of the Finance Committee had, as requested, considered the application of the lamp- lighters with respect to wages on certain holidays and as to the provision of overcoats, and that he recom- mended that three overcoats be obtained in accordance with the tenders received, and that the request as to wages be granted. " The recommendation.was approved. TELE YIELD OF WATER. _ _

It was reported at the meeting of the Water Com- mittee that the present estimated yield of water from the boreholes, well and headings, was at the rate of 499,512 gallons per day.

THE DEATH RATE. Dr. Watkins reported that the deaths were at the annual rate of 18-9 per 1,000. The rate for the first quarter of 1902 was 11-3 per 1,000, a very marked in- crease for this year. As regards matters referred to in the report of the Medical Officer of Health, it was resolved (1) that the sewer in High-street be taken up, and all drains connected with it be connected with the main sewer in the middle of the street; (2) that the Surveyor be in- structed to prepare a report as to the provision of public urinals in the district; (3) that the Surveyor and Medical Officer of Health be requested to consider the question of the escape of sewer gas and report thereon to this cOmmittee, the Surveyor to further report as to what is done in other districts with respect to the matter; (4) that the Surveyor be directed to report to this committeeas to the cost of converting. Thompson's Pit into an open-air swimming bath; and (5) that the Surveyor be also directed to prepare a report as to the oost of paving new streets with the following materials and upon the advantages or disadvantages of each material (a) tar paving (b) wood blocks and (c) stone setts.

-iiiff-dotIWCIE'§-13-oIiItOWING DIFFICULTIES.

At the meeting of the Finance Committee the chair- man of the committee reported with respect to the Local Government Board's sanction to the borrowinglof the sum of £18,679 for the purposes of the Council's water undertaking, that an application had been made to the Public Works Loan Commissioners for a loan of £13,179 to defray the cost of works which have already been done and are in course of construction, and that the Commissioners were unable to grant a loan on the security stated in the Newton-in-Makerfteld Provisional Order, 1901, until the new provisional order for amend- ing the Council's securities' had been made by the Local Government Board and confirmed by Parliament; that the Commissioners suggested that in-the meantime the Council should obtain a temporary advance to meet their present needs, and that as the Local Government Board had sanctioned such temporary borrowing the clerk and he had made application to Parr's Bank, Ltd., Warrington, who had agreed to advance to the Council the sum of £9,000 on the security of the rates and re- venne of the water urriertaking, to be repaid within six months at the following rate of ihterest, viz. :-4,1 per cent, for the first three months and if the money be required for a further throe months the interest to be at the rate nf 5 per cent. for the latter period; that the clerk and he had—on behalf of the Council—,accepted those terms, and that the loan was advanced and paid to the treasurer on the 31st March last. The Clerk submitted to the committee form of under- taking to be entered into by the Council for the repay- ment of the above-mentioned loan, together with in- terest thereon as above stated.

It was resolved that the action of the chairman of this committee and the clerk be approved and adopted, and that the Council be recommended to affix their common seal to the form of undertaking. This was agreed to.

GOOD TEMPI. ARY.

The South West Lancashire Juvenile District Council held its quarterly meeting on Saturday May 2nd at the Temperance Institute, Southport. The D.S.J.T. Bro. E. Newman presided. A large attendance of representa- tives were present, from Earlestown, Ashton, Leigh, Liverpool and Bootle. The D.S.J.T. rcpbrt showed activity all over the district four new branches being opened, and others in hand shortly tc, start. Sister Mrs. Crumblehulme, P.G.S.j.T. of Southport presented, blocks and gavels to various Superintendents who had opened new temples. They had been sent by the National Council for the Temples at Ashton Tyldesley, and Liverpool. The Council decided to take a part in the United Kingdom Alliance Bazaar and also a. chil- dren's demonstration at Liverpool. Bro. Rev. J.A. Lee gave an address urging all to greater interest, in the young. Sister Mrs. Duncan P.D.V.T. gave a report of the Grand Lodge held at Sheffield. The next (juvenile) session is to be held at Liverpool in August, under the auspices of the Pembroke Lodge. Bro. John Parr c,c.T, of Leigh also gave an address. Thanks were tendered to the Southport friends for the arrangements made for the meeting.

IRISTI CONCERT AT EARLESTOWN.

Under the auspices of the local branch.. of the Irish National League a typical Trish concert was given in the Town Hall, Earlestown on Saturday evening before a large audience. Among the artistes were Mr. William Ludwig (late of. the Carl Rosa Opera Co.), and Miss Florence Ludwig. A feature of the concert was the singing of Miss Lily Foley, who Was attired in an Trish Costume of the 11th Century, and her dancieg;the Trish jigs and reels, roused the audience to the utmost en- thusiasm. Mr. Ludwig was of course the principal figure in the programme, and being in excellent voice his singing was thoroughly enjoyed. His items were "The Trish Reaper's Harvest Hymn," " Fineen the Rover," "Night closed around" (in Gaelic), "Lament for Lord Waterford," "The Munty Mayo" (in Gaelic), "The croppy boy," "Molly Rwn," `Who fears to speak of '98." The other items in the interesting bill of Tare were songs "Na colaraidh amhain alum." "Bioun cuimhue ag furail don," " Gheobhaim aris ar Cruiscin," "Maim" (with jig), and "Barney (Mem" (with jig), by Miss Lily Foley: selections of 'airs on the Irish pipes by Mr. Luke McEvoy. Songs "Rich and rare were the gems she wore," and "The Kerry dance," Miss Florence Ludwig; recitations "Phandrig Crohoore." and "Fontenoy," Mr. Joseph Hennin ; songs "Oh; Erin my Country," and "The Wearing of ithe - Green" (in Gaelic), Mr. James Foye. The singing of "God save Ireland," terminated the concert.

THE ANNUAL REPORT.

Alderman Sureos read the annual report of the Association. The Cotincilstated thattheyhadithe pleasure in the first place of stating that the work connected with the registration of voterswas again duly attended to and resulted in a net Conservative gain of 85 on the Parliamentary register aud 91 on the municipal regis- ter, which it was believed put the party in a still stronger position on both registers than ever. In November there were municipal contests in six out of the nine wards in the borough when „the Non-trading party succeeded in gaining four seats, the number of Unionists in the Town Council being reduced by two. The only regrettable circumstance connected with the result of the elections was the fact that the Corporation and the public ' had been de- prived of the really valuable services of some of the most useful members the Town Council ever had, and their loss had already been much felt. The usual annual meetings of the various Ward committees had been held in accordance with the rules of the Associa- tion. The attendances at these meetings had, however, been very small, and the Council strongly desired to point out that unless greater interest was taken in the proceedings by the captains and members of these com- mittees it would be impossible to nngutain the organiz- ation of the party in a proper state of efficiency. The Conservative Clubs were still in a prosperous condition, and the commitkees of the clubs by their continued efforts to provide social and recreative enjoyments rendered most valuable help to the association in bringing together a continually increasing number 'of members. Theassociation had to lament the loss by death during the past year of several aid and valuable members. It was consequently of the highest import- ance that every member should assist as far as he possibly could to successfully continue the useful work in which the departed members took such a deep interest The Unionist party and thenation suffereda severe loss in the resignation of Lord Salisbury as prime minister but were fortunate in securing the Bight Hon. A. J. Balfour M.P., as his successor, and the Council had every reason to believe, that notwithstanding the severe criticisms of their opponents, the foreign and domestic policy of the present Government was gradually becoming better understood and ap- preciated throughout the country and further that the full confidence which the Conservative party still had in their ability to conduct the affairs of the nation with dignity and honour and for the best interests of its people was being largely shared in by nearly all classes of the community. The report concluded with a refer- ence to the termination of the Boer warand the corona- tion of King Edward VII. _ . .

The Ho. TREASURER (Mr. Councillor Arthur Bennett) stated that theassociation ended the year with a small balance on the right side. He pleaded for more subscribers in eider to make the association's financial position as strong as possible. The CHAIRMAN said he considered the report a most satisfactory one, but he reminded them that there was plenty of work still to bedone. In times of peacethey were all apt to get a little slack, and death having robbed them of some of their old and valued members it was all the more necessary for thoSe who held re- sponsible positions to redouble their energies. He hoped they would continue to be a united party in Warrington, and when the contest came they would re- turn their member by a larger majority than ever. (Applause). He moved the adoption of the report. Alderman FRANCOMB, in seconding the resolution, said he considered their party was in a very flourishing condition. He thought at one time • that 'they were trying to get mita office as soon as they could. The corn tax,however good of it might be,wits a frightful mis- take politically. (Hear, hear.) IT that tax had not been remitted it would have been found one of the principal planks in the Radical platform at the next general election, and would have had a great effect on the working class votes: (Hear, hear.) There was the licensing question too. He considered it was unfair to take ',Away licences without giving compensation. (Applause). The publicans were true to the Conserva- tive party, and if for some fad or other a public-house had to be closed and a man turned out into the street without receiving a penny compensation, it was a very cruel practice mid utterly un-English. In regard to the next election at Warrington, they had get a very good man for an opponentand he was such an excellent friend of Mr. Pierpoint's that to see them atthe same meeting one would almost think that the constituency ought to be divided and each gentleman returned'. (Laughter.) Mr. Crosfield was not neglecting a single opportunityof winning votes. He was for ever on the War-path and if they were not up and doing they would only win by a very short head. He did not think they ought to settle down quietly to that, but they mustputon theirarmour 1 and be prepared so that when they had. to light they I would be in a position to win. (Applause.) He hoped 1 before the next, election came round that Mr. Crosfield Iwould receive a call to some other constituency where he could win a seat. He (Alderman Francomb) would very much like to see him returned but they did not want him to represent Warrington. He hoped they would not have Mr. Crosfield as an opponent because after all he was a Warrington manand if he worked as ihe was doingat present it must tell somewhere. Tt was I only because he did not want to see their . majority 1 decrease that he would like to see Mr. Crosfield I returned for some other town.

Mr. ASHCROFT said something would have to be done or they would lose the seat. They had had miserable ward meetings, having had great difficulty in getting the workers to attend. The member would have to give more time to visiting the constituency than he had done.

The report was adopted. Alderman HUTCHINSON proposed, and Councillor W. J. FORSHA.W seconded, a vote of thanks to the officers which was heartily accorded.

South Lancashire Tramway Bill.

A Committee of the House of -Commons on Wednes- day, found the preamble. of the South Lancashire Tramways Bill proved. They granted the extension of time asked for, and agreed to amend the Act of 1900 with reference to running powers in St. Helens. Their decision was that the 'promoters should have running powers during the period that the tramway,s in St. Helens were leased to a company, but that they should only be used for passenger and light parcels traffic.

liorthwich and Warrindton Light Railway. On Wednesday the Board of Trade order was re- ceived confirminp. the Warring-NI atid Northwich Light Railway. The order includes the alterations sought for by the Northwich Council, guarding against damage from subsidence, paving for navigation, road- widening, &e. The Warrington Corporation's length of the railway has already been sanctioned, so that there is now nothing to prevent- the carrying out of the project, which is expected to open up a consider- able agricultural district. Sale of Work.

A sale of work was held in connection with Bold- street Busy Bee in the Wesleyan Schoolroom, Bold-street, on Friday, and was attended with success. It was opened by the President, Mrs. Carnson, who, in a few well-chosen words, explained the object of the sale, namely, to raise funds for the support of the little Indian girl, "Annie Philips," of Royapettah, Madras. The room was charmingly decorated, and during the afternoon Miss Purr paid a visit to the sale and made some purchases. The stallholders. were as follows :--Sewing stall, 'Miss Gill, Miss Ashdown, assisted by the Busy Bees; flower stall, Miss Ethel Robinson and Miss Ilinston ; toffee stall, Miss Maribn Weaver and Miss .1;1-awes Parkinson ; refreshment stall and tea room, Miss Barlow, Airs. W. Silcock, Miss Holt, Miss F. Wilson, and a staff of young lady helpers A palmist was in attendance during the evening, and did a brisk business, with those anxious to have their characters read.
