PROPOSED BRANCH LIBRARIES AT ALDERMANIC ELECTION. WARRINGTON. ; Councillor Dodcis—Attacks the Valuable Suggestions by Councillor ' Agreement. Upson.

Councillor UPSON introduced the motion at the meet- ing of the Warrington Town Council on Teesday even- ing requesting the Museum Committee to consider and report, on the advisability of establishing branch cir- culating libraries in different parts of the Borough. In moving the motion . which stood in his name, Councillor Upson observed that he did not think it would be a contentious motion. The time had coate,he said, when attention should be called to the fact that, the usefulness of the library might be very largely ex- tended. Warrington had grown very rapidly and was spreading out its arms in all directions so that many parts of the Borough were a considerable distance from the library. He maintained that it was too much to expect that a man after a hard day's work should walk 11 miles in order to change a book. The Corporation ought to bring within easy reach of every man in the Borough the books of the library. (Hear, hear.) The best way to do that was to establish branch libraries on the outskirts of the Borough. In the present state of the rates and ttte Borough indebtedness it was perfectly hopeless to advocate the idea of erect- ing branch library buildings. The cost of such libraries was very largely in the site and building. The cost of maintenance would be very light. The fmestion arose whether there were not buildings in existence which might be used for the purpose. Under the new Educa- tion Act the Corporation had control of the schools in the Borough and could if they wished use the schools for three nights a week. He did not knowwhether it would not be straining the provisions of the Act to use the schools /IS branch libraries on certain nights of the week but he fancied the managers would not object to lend their schools for the purpose for a small rent. He would suggest that four schools be taken situated in the north, south, east, mad west Of the town. A cupboard should be placed in each of these schools c able of accommodatina. 500 to 1,000 books. The responsible person alloteeto the " work would be glad to earn a little extra salary bv attend- ing at night to change the books, and he would suggest that the branch library be opened three nights a week. If a book that was wanted was not at the distributing centre, the clerk would take a note of it and get it . from the central library ready for the next time it was " open. By that means they might very easily extend the usefulness of the library. (Hear, hear.)Alderman Monks objected to the proposal to establish bowling greens on the ground that the mover of the reso- lution had not brought an estimate of the probable cost. He (Councillor Upson) had worked ' this matter out,and he throught these branch circulating libraries might be established' very economically. If theypaid t:10 a year for each school as rent, £l5 for a cupboard and £25 for a clerk, and c..:50 for new books, they would get an outside estimate of £lOO for each distributing centre. After the first year there would be no charge for a cupboard and is less sum for new books, so that he considered £3OO would be an outside estimate for four distributing centres. For this expendi- ture the ratepayers would get a tremendous amount of enjoyment and good. He did not ,know that this scheme had been adopted any- where else. We were charged with being a sleepy town. He thought they should have the credit of being the pioneers In England in this method of extending the usefulness of the library by economically using the schools as book distributiug centres.The charge was often made against workingmen that they, spent more time then was good for their health and pockets in certain places. He did not blame them. He thought they as a Corporation ought to give thema chance of goinc, to those places where they could enlarge and instruct minds. The idea might be enlarged by the committee taking a classroom and equipping it as a reading room and making it a place where papers could be consulted and a pleasant hour spent by men who were driven out of their homes by domestic arrangements. (Hear, hear,) Councillor DODDS seconded the resolution. Forty years ago, he said, he was himself seeretaryof a branch library, not in a to wn,but in a large parish where tliey had decided to lend part of the books to the smaller schools within a radius of three miles. Not only was the library-lan advantage to the community but he (Coun- cillor Dodds) gained many advantages himself by read- ing the books while acting as assistant librarian. He thought the suggestion of Councillor Upson an admirable one.

--- Alderman SurroN whilst symplthising with the views of Councillor Upson thought there were many diffi- culties in the way of establishing branch circul ding libraries. In the first place the expense would be very much greater than Councillor Upson imagined. He thought the cost had been under-estimated as £3oa year would not represent the cost of each branch library. Councillor UPSON : I said £lOO.

Alderman SUTTON: I don't think that would repre- sent the cost. Continuing he said there would be applications for increase of salarieS applications for a larger number of books and other matters which would make the expenditure .involved a very serious matter indeed. ,With reference to the library he asked whether there wah one workingman who did not go into the town at least once a week. He (Aldermln Sutton) did not think borrowers of books wouli bo satisfied with the sele,ltion of so few books as would be found at the branch libraries. The matter ought to be fully con- sidered and a report presented before any steps were taken.

The MAYOR pointed not that the motion of Council- lor Upson only went so far as to request the Museum Committee to consider and report on the advisability of establishing, branch circulating libraries. Alderman MONKS said the Museum Committee would be very glad of the opportunity of considering this matter and reporting to the Council. He only wanted toy this, that there was not the same reason for having branch libraries in Warrington as there w'as in many towns because the area in proportion to population was less than any other town in Lancashire or Yorkshire. He did not think 11- miles was very far to travel, especially as many had to come into town every week. The matter had been before the committee and discussed informally but no harm would be done in considering the matter again. _

—Councillor-BEsxgrr observed that, the zeal for reading vias usually not so great as to Induce a person to walk 11 miles in order to change a book. Many would walk any distance to cheer a football team, and there were others who were extremely fond of-billiards, hut he did not think they had the sameenthusiasni. for at good book. He was afraid the literature wanted by many people was sporting papers. (Laughter.)- Nevertheless the members of the Council ought to do all they could to encourage the people to read. Councillor. Upson was not only a progressive, but also a practical member of the Council. Conncillor Upson had brought forward a scheme which bore evidence of having been well thought out. He (Councillor Bennett) would like Warrington to be identified with this innovation of establishing branch' libraries at the schools. Warring. 'ton, as many of Ahem knew, was the first town in the coustry to establish a rate supported library and he ! would like them to be the first to establish branch libraries at schools. He thought the suggestion of Councillor Upson would meet a difficulty. (Hear, hear). , Councillor PEMBEirro\c thought -the suggestion with reference to establishing branch reading .rooms would be very much appreciated by workingmen. To-day we had cheap pipers and plenty of them but there was no doubt that there were manyworkingmen who could not afford to buy them. He knew from personal experience many years ago, when he (Councillor Pemberton) was a. workingman, that a branch library and rending room were of great advantage to him. Councillor UPsox, in reply, did not, think that Alder- man Sutton's argument about the expense was a very serious one. He thought he had considered thequestion of cost all round and he did not believe that. the cost would be £lOO per annum for each centre. As to the argument of distance he contended that it was a very long way indeed for anyone to have to walk front the top end of Winwick-road, kuntsford-road, Manches- ter-road on a bitter winter's night. People had often told him that they were anxiously awaiting the estab- lishment of branch libraries,and he thought they ought to provide the opportunity for people to secure a change of reading. 'it was not necessary to fit out these branch libraries with fiction. ,The probability wasthat if a man came into the branch library and saw on the. shelf books of travel, biography and poetry he might take them and thus acquire a taste for good literature At any rate if he preferred fiction and would make his request known, the book he desired could be obtained on the following night. (Hear, hear.) • The resolution was then carried. •

I The agenda for the Warrington Town Council I meeting on Tuesday evening contained the following motion in the name of Councillor Dodds :—" That in all future elections of aldermen the members of the Council shall not be bound byaayagreement which they • may have signed to observe the rule as to seniority, but each member shall be unfettered and may vote for ! anyone he pleases." • before the resolution was proposed, Alderman Surrox said he had not the slightest wish to rmt any difficulties / in the way of Councillor Dodds bringing the motion, but he respectfully submitted that it was ont of order and ' inadmissible for the reason that the Council never had anything to do with the agreement and there was nothing on the minutes of the Council respectinf, it. -It was • entirely a private agreement between individual mem- bers of the Council, and he maintained that every member who signed that agreement was perfectly free to withdraw his name from it, and was not in any way bound by it. Councillor DODDS said he was going to make some • similar suggestion. So \!ar as he understood,all the members felt that they w re perfectly at liberty to vote as they liked. If that was understood he was satisfied. Alderinan SUTTON; So far as the 'resolution is con- cerned I am quite indifferent. I Councillor DODDS: I will move it as a matter of ' form.

Councillor PEMBefrroN seconded. - Alderman SUTTON still argued that the. motion was out of order.

Councillor DODDS said he joined issue with the Alder- man. He simplypat the motion down as an expressiOii. of opipion, which he was quite entitled to do. The MA.YOR: My view is that the resolution wilt leave the matter where it finds it. Councillor PEMBERTON said there were members who would still believe in the agreement unless it was broken. How was it that the officials of the Corporation putthe agreement before members of the Council when they were elected, if it was no part of the Corporation pro- ceedings. The resolution ought to be passed and there ought to be no opposition if it was as Alderman. Sutton said.

Alderman Strrrox maintained that it was out of order and against the rules and standing orders of the Corpora- tion. The resolution was an arrangement between the two parties. • Councillor TINNION: No it =snot. Alderinan Scrros: BAween ialividual Members of the Council.

Councillor TINNION : Yes. He went on to say that in answer to the first question by Councillor Pember- ton, the Town Clerk said it was an I..greement between_ the two political parties. That was not so. The leaders of the parties were then the leaders of the Council. They had a political atmosphere in the Council, and the leaders there, without consulting their parties aft side made the agreement. Alderman Sutton was one anclAir. Henry Roberts'another, and they brought this agreement forward with the intention of removing the very serious differences in the Council. He (Councillor Tinnion) hacl been accused :repeatedly of preaching the doctrine: "Once an Alderman, always an Alderman," but he had never preached such a doctrine. SO long as their signatures remained attached- to that agreement political feeling ran high, and it was thought desirable to get rid of it so far as possible. He did not hesitate to say that there were men returned to the Council who beat men in a political fight who would have made much better councillors.' Probably if he had known as much about the agreement as he did now he might. not have supported ib. It had, however, done good. Those of them who remembered the incrimi- nations at that time 'n'euld be glad to see the much happier way business was conducted now. He should not vote for the resolution nor allow Councillor Dodds to provide a bridge for him. When he found he could not conscientiously net according to the agree- ment he would give notice to the Town Clerk to with- draw his name. He thought it was altogether unneces- sary, though very kind, 01 Councillor Dodds to provide this means. Thc more straightforward way was to. agree with Mr. Dodds mod give notice to the Town Clerk repudiating the agreement. There were men in the Council—himself among them—who were seriously considering their position. They were so wilfully mis- understood that it was not worth while giving the best of their brain and time in the service of the town.

The TOWN CLERK: I am obliged to Councillor Tinnion tor calling myattention to the slight mistake I made. Ti, would have been better if j had said "leaders of the Council."

Councillor Dorms : After Councillor Tinnion's sp,lech I must insist on a vote being taken. He had said tho agreement did not mean "Once an alderman always art alderman." If the word '.re-election "in the agreement: did not mean that they were to vote for them for ever he did not understand the English language. Councillor TINNION : Tt, does not, mean thatt to me.

Councillor Donn It might be-heT;:a..s. not alluding to any present aldermln—though something serious might be alleged against one of these gentlemen whom they were to elect for ever. Yet such men could still coma and claim to be elected aldermen.

Councillor TINNION : Nothing of the sort. I cai give notice at any time and the agreement would not be binding. • Councillor DODDS said, in regard to new members signing the agreement, he knew something of party— working in other towns. They got these weak men in for their :strong wards. They got men who might be dummies, and yet these men were certain, under such an agreement, to be aldermen eventually. He alluded to Councillor Pierpoint as an instance. He wasa strong man who would probably win any ward four times out of five and the fifth time he might be defeated. Con- sequently he would never be an. alderman. and yet he might be the ablest man in the Council. These were his strong objections to theagreement,and he asked for a vote to be taken.

The Tows CLERK: Neither the agreement nor the resolution had any legal effect or meaning. It wasa matter of consciencefor each individual member con- cerned. A member might vote in any way he pleased- The motion was then put to the vote. For it there voted : Councillor Bennett, Hulse, Barton, Forshaw, Brophy, John Roberts, Dodds, Lard, Pemberton, and Upson. Councillor Tinnion alone voted against, Alderman Surros and Councillor JAMES EVANS remarking that they were quite indifferent.l The resolution was therefore carried.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

FACTORY ACT PROSECUTIONS AT WARRINGTON. At the Warrington Police Court on Friday, before Mr H. Roberts and other magistrates, the Whitecross Com- pany, Ltd., were summoned for employing five young persons beyond the hours required by the Factory Acts. —Major Roe, Chief Factory Inspector for the district, prosecuted and said the boys were working at 6-50 p.m. on April 7th, whereas they should have left work at 6 o'clock. On February 27th the Company were con- victed a similar offence.—Mr. Steel appeared for the Company and said the work was let to employees on contract and the boys were employed by work men and not directly by the firm. Recently a man had been employed by the company to stand at the entrance of the works and prevent boys from going in after working hours. On the date in question the man had been called away to work at a galvanizing vat. The directors had determined that the manager of the department must pay the penalty in- flicted, and he therefore asked the Bench to take a lenient view of the offence. The magistrates imposed a fine of 20s. and cost in each case—The United Velvet Cutters Association Limited; were summoned for work_ ing a girl named Alice Green, 16 years of age. during the dinner hour on March 10th—Mr • Steel defended —lnspector Eatock stated that he visited the defendants' workshop in Bostock- street at 12.40 on the morning in question. The dinnenhour was from 12 to one o'clock, but the girl Green was working.—Evidence was given by the girl who stated that the foreman had to go an errand so he left the key of the workshop with her and told ber to close the place at 12.30. He did not know she was going to work during the dinner hour.—Mr. Steel stated that a notice was displayed in the workshop forbidding employes to work during meal times. The Associa- tion was not responsible for the breach of the Act and he asked the Bench to grant a summons against the foreman and make him bear the penalty.—The Bench refused to adopt this course and fined the defendants in the costs.

• #RlDAY.—Before Mr. H. Roberts (in the chair), Alder- men Smethurst and Monks, Councillor John Roberts, Messrs. Hepherd, Thornton, and Young. T- ROUBLESOME BOYS.—Three boys named Henry Chad- wick, 5, Dial-court; John Higham, 4, Bay Horse-yard; and Henry Bate, 5, Arnold's-yard, were summoned for making loud and unseemly noises in the Market Hall on 14th April.—P.C. Clough said the boys were shout- ing and jumping and swinging on the stalls.—lnspector Place said very much,damagewas done in the Market by that kind of conduct, and it was becoming a perfect nuisance.—Mr Birchall suggested putting a notice board up, and the Chief Constable remarked that offenders would" improve the case by pulling it down." (Laugh- ter.)—The Chairman thought the marketcould be railed. —Defendants were lined ss. each. \ CRUELTY TO A HORSE.—William George Scragg, Oaklandeye Farm, Thelwell, was summoned for illtreat- ing a horse by causing it to be worked while in an unfit state on• April 21st—P.C. Heap stated that he was in company with P.C.Hayes, and saw the animal attached to a milk cart. As it appeared to be very lame they stopped it and found it very weak on the off fore leg. Tt was subsequently examined by Inspector Print, who found it lame on both forelegs and on the near hind one. —A fine of 10s.was imposed. LODGERS ROBBED.—Honry Caffney, was charged with 'stealing a. watch and chainand medal, a pair of boots, two pairs of socks and a singlet, of the value of £1 10s. 6d from the lodging-house of Mary Conway,Union- street.—The Chief Constable stated that there were several men staying in the lodging-house on the night of the theft, and prisoner went to bed, when the others had already retired. The next morning Caney left the house before the others got up and several lodgers missed the articles named. Detectives Forster and Meacocke traced the prisoner to Preston Brook, where he was trying to dispose of the stolen articles at farms and public-houses. They lost trace of him, however,but subsequently struck another clue and he was arrested at Cheadle in Staffordshire on Friday. He was brought to Warrington and was wearing the boots and a pair of the stolen socks. He had disposed of the other articles at Mumma and other plaoes.—He had been 13 times con- victed, and was an inveterate, lodging-house thief.— Prisoner was sent to gaol for three months with hard labour.

Earlestown Occasional Court.

DRUNK AND DiSORDERLY.—Before Mr. J. W.Emmett on Monday, Thomas Riley and Joseph Vance, labourers, of Burtonwood, were charged with being drunk and disorderly at Earlestown on Saturday. P.C. Hughes supported the charge and both men were fined 2.5. 6d. and costs each.

THEFT OF A SHAWL.—Riottard Dobson, of no fixed residence, was4charged with stealing a shawl from the shop of Mr. Jones, pawnbroker, Market-street, Earles- town, on Saturday, of the value of 5s.—P.C. Mellor s tated that on Saturday afternoon he met the prisoner in Market-street, carrying something bulky under his j acket. Witness stopped him and asked him what he had under his arm, but not receiving a satisfactory ex- planation he was taken to the police station and in- quiries were made. Mr. Henry Hockenhull, manager for Mr. Jones, stated that the shawl was safe in the shop on Saturday morning rend he identified it as his pro- perty. Inspector Foster in asking for a remand, stated that the prisoner was an old thief and had a large number of previous convictions, twelve of which were for felony. Prisoner was remanded until Thursday to the Warrington County Police Court.

CHAMPION ARMY CLOG DANCER.

A Lancashire Man's Feat.

There are few better known clog dancers in Lancashire than Mr. Hugh Sloan, with wham a Press representa- tive recently had an interesting interview at 3, Charnock-street, Bolton. Apprenticed as a fitter, young Hugh, like a good many others, developed a taste for soldiering, and immediately he was at liberty to do so left his mechanic's bench and enlisted in the West Yorkshire Regiment. With his battalion he visited various military depots abroad, being for some time stationed at Malta. One of his favourite recreaiions was clog dancing in the old Lancashire style, and his feats of endurance soon brought him into prominence with his comrades.

===== 0002601_19030508_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
FL HUGH SLOAN.

(Front a photograph.) But: the rigours of a military life abroad told upon him, and he gradually became aware of the fact that his muscular system was not so free in action as formerly, At last a settled pain across the shoulders manifested ittelf, and finally he was ordered to the Military Hospital. Everything was there done to clear his system of the rheumatic puns, but all to no purpose, and he had to be invalided from the army. Mr. Sloan returned to his native town, where he en- deavoured to follow his old occupition—fitting. But the trouble with the back kept reourring, causing him to temporarilyreliaquish his employment,but being naturally of good spirits, in the intervals between the attacks, he cultivated his love for clog and skate da,ncing,and became xo proficient that his services were in great demand up and down the country. At Leeds he entered a competition for time dineing, and carried of! the first prize, having danced an hour and a quarter without cessation—a wonderful feat of endur- ance. The tremendous exertion, however, cost him door. The strain was too great, and the victor returned once more to Bolton broken in health. His subsequent care by Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people is best told in his own words: _ _ .

wv, •• •• •• • "The colour went from my face, my cheeks fell in, and my muscles turned flabby. I felt thoroughly ill and run down, my appetite went, and the pains across my shoulders and down my back wore so severe at nights that I could only get sleep by lying on my chest. "Occasionallil would seem to pick up a little, tuad on one such occasion I accepted an engagement at Chorley. There I mentioned my condition to a gentleman who told me what good Pr. Williams' pink pills for pale people had done for him, and he recommended me'to try them.

-7`l"got a box,and by the time I had emptied it the , pains were sufficiently relieved to allow me to sleep at I night. My appetite too was noticeably returning. ! "I persisted with a second and a third box, and then I ; felt I was cured. Now lam first-class in health and never felt better. What has been troubling me on and ; off for about five years has been entirely eradicated."

Warrington Polyglot Society.

The Warrington Polyglot Society held the first of a series of summer excursions on Saturday last, and though the conditions were not propitious for the start, the refit of the day was all that could be desired. By cycle, carriage and train, some twenty members and friends journeyed to Halton Castle and spent the afternoon among the ruins, while some of the party indulged in bowls, the green being in fair condition, as Halton escaped the heavy rainfall which rendered the day so disagreeable in Warrington. After tea the business side of the excursion received due recogni- tion, Mr. Haviour reading a paper in French on the history of the Castle, the interesting details of which were supplemented by Dr. Markel and Mr. Brookes. Business con,,luded, the party alljOurned to the green and the ladies took part in the bowling with en- thusiasm and no mean skill, until at eight o'clock the .members loft the interesting ruins behind and re- turned to Warrington by road and rail. If it be ,generally true that we "take our pleasures sadly." in this country, the Polyglot Society under the influence possibly of its continental friends, has learned to "change all that," thus adding to the debt already owing to those who have given such valuable assist- ance to Warringtonians in the acquisition of foreign languages. Certainly the excursion was most enjoy- able, and the members are looking forward to their next expedition, which will probably be to Northwich with the object of exploring a salt mine.

The number in the cor- responding week of last year was 88, showing a decrease of 18, beinga net decrease in 1903, to date, of 10.

WARRINGTON TO-,'121 COUNCIL.

The monthly meeting of the Council was held at the Town Hall on Tuesday evening when there were present: —The Mayor (Mr. J. C. Parr), the Deputy-Mayor (Alderman .J Fairclough), Aldermen T. Hutchinson,F. W. Monks, J. Smethurst, I'. H. Sutton, Councillors T Barton, A. Bennett, J. Brophy, J. G. Craik, J. Dodds, James Evans, L. Ellison, W. J. Forshaw, T. J. Hulse, J. Lord, A. Peabock, J. Pemberton, J. Roberts, William Roberts, W. Rogers,D. Tinnion, J. Upson, J. Wilkinson, with Messrs. J. L. Whittle (town clerk), S. Wilson (deputy town clerk), J. Fairhurst (borough treasurer), T. Longdin(boroughsurveyor),J. Dees (water engineer), W: H. Grimsdale (electrical engineer), and J. M.Murray (director of education.) ALDERMAN SHAW GREEN'S RESIGNATION.

THE COUNCIL'S REGRET.

The following Jotter was read from Alderman Shaw Green resigning the office of alderman:—" Dear Mr. Mayor,—l didnot expect to write this letter for many years to come, but my medical adviser and medical friends all tell me that the time has arrived when I must. I have ever taken an earnest interest in the I affairs of my native town, and I had even anticipated the possibility of some day occupying the chair you so ; worthily fill, so that you will not be surprised when I say that I do most sincerely regret having to write it. The work has always been asource of pleasure,for I did it from a sense of duty,balieving that those who could, should give some portion of their ability to the public serviee. I rejoice to think that I have been privileged to take part in our great developments, and no one can better realize the difference in time and energy required from the members now,to what it was at the time I entered the Council some sixteen years ago. During the whole of this period I have had most generous treatment at the hands of my ;olleagues; and my inter- course wllff the officials has been of a most agreeable character. In placing in your hands my resignation o the office I hold as an Alderman of the Borough, will you please to convey to the Council and the officials my warmest thanks for all their kindness ti me ?—I am, dear Mr. Mayor, yours faithfully (Signed). J. SHAW GREEN."

The MAYOR said he could not better express his feel- ings than by reading his reply which he had sent by return. The letter read as follows Town Hall April 23rd, 1903. Dear Mr. Alderman Shaw Green,—l have received your letter this morning with very deep and sincere regret both on account of the resignation it embodiee and the reasons for it. I had hoped that rest and relaxatioa might have restored the health which has been failing you of late, as your collealpes have noticed with the greatest concern and sympathy, but I feel that your decision is irrevocable on the grounds you give, and I would, therefore, not add to your trouble by urging its reconsideration. As Mayor I feel that I am losing one of the ablest and most efficient and painstaking members of the Council, and upon whose sound judgment and clear intellectual faculties I felt the greatest reliance, and as a personal friend and coun- sellor I feel that your absence will be a loss not easily estimated andhardly to be replaced. I sincerely hops that a temporary break from your duties and responsi- bilities may effect a complete restoration to healthend that we may still cherish the hope that your serviette are only interrupted and not lost to the town.' (Hear, hear.)—With kind sympathy and cordial regret, believe me, yours faithfully (signed), J. CHARLTON PARR, Mayor. Alderman Surrox said he would like to take that opportunity of saying that in his experience he had never known a more capable or valuable member of the Council than Alderman Shaw Green. (gear, hear). Whatever he undertook to do he made up his mind to do well, and in a manner which should reflect credit upon himself. As chairman of the Finance Committee he did not think that they had ever had his equal. (Hear, hear). His professional ability and experience enabled him to grasp every difficult point and to put it before them in a way which they could easily under- stand. His reports and explanations with reference to all financial matters cost him much thought and labour. During the fifteen years Alderman Shaw Green sat on the Council,he also took an active part in other depart- ments, more particularly its chairman of the School Attendance Committee. His heart was in the work,and In every instance he felt that they had sustained a great loss. Their loss was very heavy indeed. They might,he thought, express the hope that with the rest he was now taking he would soon be restored to good health (Hear, hear). „ Alderman SMETHURST saie he could scarcely allow that occasion to pass without expressing his deeparegret at the 'cause of Alderman Shaw Green having to leave that Council. He had worked with him shoulder to shoulder for upwards of 12 years. Heserved under him some seven years onthe School Attendance Committee, and he was sure the loss to that committee of a gentle- man who conducted the business with such affection and with such regard to the poor people of the town was a loss which he,was sure could not be told. (Hear, hear.) So far as that Council was concerned he could only endorse the words that the Mayor and Alderman Sutton had expressed. Alderman Shaw Green's resignation was not only a loss to that Council but to the whole borough of Warrington. They might get as good a man again to fill the position offchairman of the Finance Committee, but the devotion and time Alderman Shaw Green must have given to that office, must have been very great. He was one of those gentlemen who, when he brought forward the Budget—for which they waited so anxiously—feared no man and no committee. He just said what he had to say, and used to dress them down sometimes very severely, but he thought that they were none the worse for it. Some of .them required to be kept in check a little when they got upon matters of finance, and Aldermen Shaw Green was worthy of all the honour and praise that Council could give him for the services he had rendered to that Council and the town at large during the last 15 years. (Hear. hear.) Councillor WiLmam ROBERTS said he would like, RS one of the younger members, to say one word upon that very sad occasion. He thought the cause of their friend's retirement was one that must be regretted by them all. To those who knew the alderman intimately,it was no secret that he had worked early and late at his own business in order that he might give the time required for his public duties, and, as they knew, he had given his time unstintingly in the public service. They could not accept that resignation without also expressing their sympathy with Mrs. Green and family in this sore trial. 'lt was every sore trial, and they could only ex- press the hope that Alderman Shaw Green's health would soon be comnletell restored. (Hear, hear.) Councillor TINNION said he could not add anything to the admirable letter that the Mayor had sent in reply, nor to the speeches that had been made, but he thought it would be only right and in harmony with the feelings of the Council if a resolution were recorded acknowledg- ing the services that had been rendered to that town by a gentleman whom he could only describe as one of the most industrious and most conscientious members, either of the present or any past Corporation. The Mavort said he proposed to move a formal reso- lution.

Councillor Itoomas said he had pleasure in supporting every word that had been spoken. He had sat vv.ith Alderman Shaw Green for nearly eight years on the School Attendance Committee and a more straightfor- ward chairman it, would he impossible to find,

The MAYOR then moved "That this Council receives with regret the resignation of Joseph Shaw Green,Esq., of the office of alderman and desires to place on recor4. the appreciation of the eminently valuable services rendered by him in a connection withAthe Council of upwards of 15 years." The DEPUTY-MAYOR seconded. T.n doing so, he said it needed no words of his to recommend theinotion to them. It had been undoubtedly a very great blow to Mr. Shaw Green to have to retire. The motion was then carried unanimously. The MAYOR thenjormally declared the Aldermanic seat vacant.

PAVING AND SEWERAGE COI MITTEE.

A QUESTION OF THE "LOWEST TENDER." Ata meeting of the Paving and Sewerage Corn mittee it was resolved that the tender of Messrs. Bennie and Thompson for the forming and paving of the under- mentioned streets or passages according to specification be accepted :—Passage from gable of 114, Manchester- road to gable of 57, Willis-street, at £l3O 14s. 7d.; pass- age behind 41 to 89, Birchall-street, at £2lO 16s. ' • pass- age between 57 and 59, Birchall-street, at £l7 10s. 4d.: passage between 75 and 77, Birchall-street, at £lB 68.2 d. It was also resolved that the tender of Messrs. Buckley and Company, Limited, for carting for the Scavenging Department at 7s. 6d. per day, and the tender of Messrs. Denhaafand Company fer the Highway Department at 7s. 2d. per day for 12 months according to the terms of the specification be accepted. Councillor TINNION called attention to the paving of the passages behind Birchall-street, and said he might explain that it had betal the practice of the committee lin dealing with the tenders to except the lowest, but on that ocpasion they had thought it desirable and in I the best interests of the work not to accept exactly the I lowest tender. One tender for the passage behind 41 to 89, Birchall-stteet was for £2lO 16s. and another £2lO 95., a difference of seven shillings. The next was for 57 :tnd 59, Birchall-street, and one tender was for I£l7 10s. 4d. and the other £l7 Bs. 6d., the difference being is. 10d. The next tender for practically the same passage or a portion of It was £lB 6s. 1:14 and the other for £3O 14s. 3d. The explanation he wished to give was that it was not thought desirable for a matter of 115 s. that two contractors should be at work on what was practically the same passage. Alderman Huratissox seconded.

Councillor PEMBERTON moved an amendment, and remarked that Councillor Tinniou had not made the matter quite as dear as he might have done. It was rather misletding to say that one tender was for £lB 6s. 2d. and the other A3O because there was another tender submitted for £lB for the last job. The principle of the committee ought to be to accept the lowesttender when the contractor was capable of doing the work. The contractor in question had done work previously, and there had been no complaints, and he did not think that it was in the best interests of the ratepayers theta tender should be barred out. It was grossly unfair to pick out a tender, and to say that one man should do this and the other that, when the contractors were of equal merit. It might not strike the members of the Council as being unfair, but it was. In this case it was like a man losing a race by four inches end the Judge saying he might have the prize because the other man won one earlier in the day. Counoillor W. ROBERTS: Some of us do not know anything about racing, Mr. Mayor. (Laughter). Councillor PEMBERToN: lam talking about athletic sports. (More laughter.) He added that it was heart- breaking to people who tendered if they did not get the job when they were lowest. Councillor LORD seconded the amendment.

Councillor Jamns EVANS referred to the tenders for carting, and said he did not know how they had escaped the notice of Councillor Pemberton. It seemed to him to be a more serious item than the one he had taken up. The committee had accepted a tender of Messrs. Buckley for carting at 7s. 6d. per day and also accepted the tender of Messrs. Denham and Co. at is. 2d. He took it that there was fourpence per man and horse difference, and that would make a serious item in twelve months. Perhaps some explanation would be given with refer- 'nee to the margin of fourpenee between the tenders. Councillor WILLIAM ROBERTS said he had yet to learn that the Council had accepted the principle that the lowest tender must be accepted, and if the com- mittee was not to be allowed to use its discretion in the acceptance of tenders he did not know how the Council, not having all the facts before them, could arrive at a fair decision if the committee themselves could not. On thee oi grounds he supported the committee. Councillor JOHN Ronnwrs said he was not aware that Denham and Company ever contracted for the Scaveng- ing Department. The Highway Department was the only one they tendered to. COUISCMOT TINI•UON said what the committee did was to accept the lowest tender for the Highway Depart- ment, which vvis is. 2d. per day. Messrs. Denham did not tender for the scavenging department, and if they had done so at 7.. 2d. their tender would have been accepted. A. regarded Councillor Pemberton, he could not follow him in the figures, but would like to say that there were seven different tenders. Messrs. Bennie end Thompson tendered for the whole of the work £705 12e. 2d., and T. Stringer's tenders were £709 18s. 2d. Those were the figures he had before him, and he failed to see how,if looked at in the way suggested,they would have arrived at the lowest figure on behalf of Mr. Stringer. If they had accepted positively the lowest tender it would have gone to G. T. Heaton, and as it was the whole of the tenders were divided by Messrs. Bennie and Thompson and Mr. Stringer. There were other tenders higher thsn those accepted. The only reason why the committee departed from their usual practice was not to bring a second contractor on the work, as they did not think it worth while for a few shillings difference. They were most likely to get the work better done by the one contractor. What the committee had done had been dome in the best interests of the Corporation. The amendment was pittt to the meeting and lost, only Councillor Pemberton, Lord and Dodds voting for it, and the minutes were then approved.

THE SCHOOLS DRILL COMPETITION.

A WARNING TO THE ELEMENTARY SCHOOLS. Councillor TINNION proposed the minutes of the Gymnasium Committee, which contained the following resolu;ion :—" That in consequence of only one entry having been received for the Elementary School Chil- dren's Drill Competition for the Thomas Morrie Memorial Shields, viz., from the Heathside boys, no competition be held this season and that the holders be requested to return the shields which when received be hung in the Institution." He remarked that it was a matter for very great regret that the schools had not I taken up this question, and he felt bound, on behalf of the Committee, to give them a gentle hint that if they I did not think itnecessary or desifable to go in for this competition,the Committee might not think it desirable g to give them the excellent instruction they obtained for' such a low cost at the Gymnasium. They could only provide accommodation for a certain number of schools, and in the future it might be one of the conditions that they must enter this Competition before receiving instruction from the Gymnasium.

COUNCILLOR thRD AND THE TRAMS. CounCillor LORD asked the Chairman of the Electric- ity and Tramways Committee :- 1. Why are our bars not provided with means of out- side communication as are the cars in other towns? 2. ri it not a fact that outside means of communica- tion were fixed, but have been removed, andwhy? 3. Is it notalso a fact that several ladies, owing to there being no such means of communication, have narrowly escaped accidents through having to leave their seats whilst the cars were in motion in order to request theconductor to stop the car? Alderman MONKS, with reference to the first question said the consulting engineer did not specify in the drawing up of the specificationsYor the cars that means of communication should be provided, and the committee did not think it worth while they should be included. •With regard to the seconcliquestion, he said t'...et the means of communication referred to, were placed on the cars by the :constructors without any instructions. They made the committee a I present of the work, and, unfortunately, it was expendi- ture on their part which was thrown away, becausethey found the public had misused the means of communica- tion, and not only misused them,but to the danger of the passengers, and the communications had to be taken away to prevent accidents. With respect to the third I question, he was not able to say whether it was a fact that several ladies had narrowly escaped accidents. No reports of any such accidents had been received at the I office; the manager had heard nothing about the matter. I Certainly the cOmmittee had not had the matter before them. When they remembered that the cars had Won I 'running 12 months, and the number of passengers that had been carried, and that there had been so few acci- dents, it must be admitted there was evidence that no undue risk was being run for want of communication. In fact, owiag to the small number of accidents,the in- surance company had renewed their policy for a con- siderably leas premium than they paid last year. (Hear I hear.) Councillor LORD said he was much obliged to the alderman for his replies. He supposed he was not en- titled to enter into any argument about the matter. THE ELECTRICAL AND TRAMWAY UNDER- TAKINGS. LOSS ON ELECTRICITY.—PROFIT ON TRAMWAYS.

At a meeting of the Electricity and Tramways Com- mittee the following resolutions of the Paving and Sewerage Committee were considered and approved That this committee offer the Tramways Committee the sum of £2,446, being Oats value of the setts displaced by the laying of the tram rails, subject to verification of the weights ; that the Tramways Committee be charged the sum of £1,350, being the cost of reinstating streets after the construction of the tramways, this committee having allowed the sum of £250 as the estimated value between new and old setts; that the wages of Mr. John Dunn, chief clerk, be increased by the sum ef 2s. 6d. per week, with an annual increase of 2s. 6d. to a maximum of £2 per week; and that the main in Winwick-road be extended to the Blue Coat School. _ _

Alderman MONKS moved the confirmation of the minutes.

The DEPUTY-MAYOR seconded.

Alderman MONKS said he was in possession of the profit and logs account on the electricity and tramway( undertakings. He would give the information to the Council if it was desired.

It was decided that the information should be given.

Alderman MONKS then stated that the nett result of the operations in the eleotrioity department was a loss on the year of £495 Bs. 3d. after paying all the working expenses, after providing the proper instalment of the sinking fund, interest on loan, bank interest and com- mission. If they had not made the reduction in the price of the ourrent at the commencement of the year by giving the consumers the benefit of half an hour in the demand system they would have about covered the loss. The reduotion was about equal to £439. The figures were not quite as favourable as he had been led to hope for, but next year, if all went on well, he thought the ccounts would show a profit. Councillor BENNETT asked what the gross profits had been. .

Alderman MONKS said he did notattaoh much import- ance to gross profits, since that did not allow for pay- ments of interest, sinking fund charges, etc. (Hear, hear.) The receipts, however, had been £7,912 16s. Bd., and the working expenses were about £5,100, the gross profit being £2,834 15s. 3d. With reference to the tram- ways, Alderman MONKS stated that the Corporationtmly commenced running the first system of trams in April last, but theyhad started the year with an accumulation of accounts owing for interest, &c., of £561 135., but the net results in profits had been £652 ss. 3d. (Hear, hear.) Therefore this £652 went to pay off the adverse balance with which they had started the year, and left 0012 s. 3d. to be carried forward. (Hear, hear.) _

, Councillor DODDS asked if that inCluded an allowance for depreciation on rails, cars and works? Alderman Sforucs stated that it included sinking fund and interest payments, but he:did not know what Coun- cillor Dodds meant by depreciation. Different people meant different things by depreciation. They were main- taming the service and paving all charges. Councillor BRNNErr : You contend that you main- tain as you go along? Alderman MONKS: Yes, and we are paying off a portion of the capital account.

- The minutes u:ere then carried. COUNCILLOR PEMBERTON AND THE ELECTION OP' ALDERMEN. A SERIES OF' QUESTIONS. Did your committee instruct the Town Clerk to draw up the following agreement :—"We, the under- signed members of the Warrington Town Council, here- by respectfully agree to observe the rule of seniority amongst the members of the Council in the election or re-election of the present or future aldermen of the borough." 2. Did the committee instruct the Town Clerk to place the agreement before each newly-elected member to the Town Council at the time he was signing the acceptance of office? 3. If so, did he when he so placed it before each member explain the meaning of the agreement? 4. Did he inform each member that it was op- tional?

5. Was the agreement drawn up by the Town Clerk, and if so by whose instructions? _

6. How many members of the present Council have signed the agreement ? 7. Who was the last member to sign the document and what was the date of such signature; 'rho MAYOR Slid he had no personal knowledge of the work of the General Purposes Committee at that time and he would ask the Town Clerk to reply. The TOWN CLERK then read his replies as follows ,

WHAT IS THIS SUBSTITUTION? -- How People are Defrauded.

It is high time that public attention should be drawn to a subject, which has so far only agitated manufac- turers and retail traders, yet which is one of extreme interest and practical importance to everyone who shops. It must not for a moment be thought that the practice to which attention is here called, is indulged in by the majority of shopkeepers it is not, but on the other hand it is far more prevalent than should be the case, and its adoption by a dishonest and shortsighted 'minority is not only a wrong to the pubiic but most damaging to fair and honest dealers. Substitution is the practice to which we are referring, and, as might be done with the practice of lying, it can be divided into several classes.

One of these classes is fortunately illegal. If you were to enter the shop of Blank, and ask for—" Robin- Son's Cigar & Brandy Lotion " ; if Blank were to hand you out a packet, which is either made up to look very like one of Robinson's well-known packets, or which is wrapped up so that you cannot see what you are receiving, and if Blank, the shopkeeper, does not say anything to enlighten you as to the fact that you are not being served with what you asked for, then Blank lays himself open to a suit at the hands of Robinson, the manufacturer of the lotion. But how often does Robinson hear of the offence, or succeed in running the offender to earth? He hears of but few of many such transactions, which are directly damaging to his trade, because of the lost sales, and are also indirectly injuri- ous to his good name, which suffers in consequence of the substitution for his own of other and always in- ferior goods.

There are many other forms of substitution, which do I not oome within cognisance of the Courts, but against, which the public would do well to set their face, inas- much as they are nothing less than traps to catch the unwary and to jell to them not only what they do not desire but what is too often to their detriment to perch:a.se. Let us take a case. You have gone into the shop of a "substitutor," who knows the law and does not intend to come within its clutches. You have asked for "Robinson's Cigar and Brandy Lotion"; says the shopkeeper, "Here is something else which is really just as good, and which has the additional advan- tage' of being considerably cheaper." You may be tempted by the plea "just as good" and "cheaper"; you pay your money but do not take your choice,but his. Or again it may chance that the shopkeeper will say "Robinson's Lotion ?—why I make a precisely similar thing myself." Again you may be weak enough to yield, buying what you do not intend to purchase, instead of what you did. The foregoing are two very typical cases, which it will be profitable to examine. The three parties concerned in these transactions are Robinson —the manufacturer and proprietor of a well- know article,Blank—the retail dealer, and you—one of the general .public upon which public,Blank directlyand Robinson Indirectly are dependent for their living. How has Robinson been affected by Blank's method of precedure ? In both of the two cases givenhe has lost, a sale. You, either having heard of, or having exper- ienced, the value of Robinson's article, or having read , an advertisement which inclined you to test its value, I have asked for one thing and have received another. ' The dealer's conduct, therefore, to put it very mildly, is ungenerous to Robinson, who, by the merits of his goods or the efficiency of his advertising, has brought a customer into the shop. Of course,the dealer may say, and very often does say, that he ean make but little profit from selling Robinson's goods; this may, or may not be true, but even if true it should be remembered, that Robinsonhas introduced a customer into the shop, and that the dealer if he is an astute man of business, should contrive not only to make a sale of Robinson's goods, but should also make a friend of the purchaser, induce him to become& regular customer, and probably even on his first visit sell to him something else in addi- tion to that which he came to buy. Surely it would be profitable to Blank to do all that he can to support those who bring the public into his establishment ? Robinson, we think, has a very clear grievance against him. You have been served with an article, which you are assured is "just as good "as Robinson's. It may be taken for certain that the article is not just as good" probably not nearly as good, and, if you do not person- ally know the value of Robinson's article, you will assuredly be prejudiced against it, although you have not in reality given it a trial. I But we are not concerned with Robinson, the manufac- turer, so much as with the public, to whom we will turn after devoting a few words more to the retail dealer. Blank has acted unfairly to Robinson as we have already pointed out, and as we shall point out, to you alsoamon whom his living depends. What is the motive that actuates Blank? Is it philanthropy or love of you? No, profit is the motive, a motive not unfair in itself,but we do not think that Blank's profit is fairly come by. The substitute sold is never anything like as good as the article for which it is substituted. That is to say that Blank has lied to you, in order to make more money out of you than he would have done if he had told you the , truth. He also lies in almost every case, when he sub- stitutes goods which he says are of his own manufac- ture. They are almost always not of his own making, but are bought f,rom manufacturers, who print Blank's name on the labors of the goods he orders. This dodge I is played in many trades; such manufacturers can always , afford to sell their goods at a cheap rate, as they have no need to spend any money on the careful experiments and the costly machinery for producing a newand really good article. So in almost every case the substituting tradesman is a liar, taking no count of the means by which he makes his money. He is unwise, too, for he injures both his friends Robinson and the public. You indeed, that is to say the public, have a poor time of it. You want one thing, and you are persuaded to take another; the thing that you purchase may be cheaper in price (though often it is not so), but it is actually dearer to you, because it is neither what you want nor so good; you have in most cases been lied to and deceived. Strangely enough, you will in all probability continue to patronise the shop where common senseand experience should tell you that you will not only not be able, at any rate without some trouble, to buy what you want, but will in elk probability be served with some- thing not nearly so good as what you desired to purchase. In this fact we have the clue to the cure for this evil of substitution. If the public will only face the diffi- culty and will determine only to deal with shops where they are readily and willingly served with what they as for, the substituting dealer will soon vanish from the face of the land, and honest straight-dealing trades- men will come into their own. Surely it is good public policy to encourage honest, and to discourage dis- honest. traders ?

Substitution exists, to a greater or less degree,among all classes of retail dealers. Tobacconists, spirit mer- chants, linen-drapers, chemists, provision dealers,dealers in photographic materials, dealers in mineral waters and other temperance drinks, and so on through the whole list! You must keep your eyes oped', and keep them always open, and keep them wide open. Substitu- tion is not an injury to your pocket only,but very often to your health also. We have spoken strongly, but possibly not strongly enough, bearing in mind the importance of this question to the manufacturer, to the honest majority of retail dealers, and to you and the public in general.

-About five o'clock on Monday afternoon Mr. 11. N. Roscoe, of Beswick-street, a country postman working between Warrington and Bruche, was returning on his bicycle to Warrington, and when near Woolston be was about to pass a furniture van coming in the opposite direction, when an omnibus came from behind it. He tried to avoid a collision, but his machine skidded and he was thrown off, his head strikinc, the side of the 'bus, and rendering him unronscions. He - was conveyed to Warrington in a cart; by Mr. Broad- hurst, a potato dealer, together with the mails which he was carrying. He was attended by Dr. Bowden., who found him to be badly bruised. He was after- wards taken home P.S.A. Meetings.

NEW RAILWAY APPOINTMENT AT WARRINGTON. Retirement of Mr. E. Braide.

On:Friday, Mr. T. I. Williams, of Abergavenny, com- menced his duties at Warrington as district goods manager for the London and North-Western Railway Company, in succession to Mr. Edward Braide, who has retired after almost completing fifty years of honourable and valuable railway service. For the past 29 years, Mr. Braide has been district goods manager, with headquarters at Warrington. He was of a retiring disposition and never sought to enter public life,but by those with whom he came in contact, he was highly re- spected. .To the London and North-Western Rail- way Company he has been a loyal servant and the directors have shown their anxiety that the company should continue to have the advantage of Mr. Braide's wide experience by inducing him to consent to his services being at their disposal in his retirement. Even yesterday when Mr. Braide was supposed to have "retired," an " Examiner" reporter found on enquiry , at his residence in Bewsey-road that he had gone to Fleetwood on business connected with the Company. In entering upon a period of retirement from his long and arduous duties we feel sure that Mr. Braide will carry with him the heartiest wishes of his friends that he may have health and strength to enjoy it. The clerical staff at the district goods office intend to take any early opportunity of showing their regard for their late chief by presenting him with a solid silver tea and coffee service and tray. -Mrs. litaide is also to receive a silver rose bowl.

Mr. T. I. Williams, who commenced his duties yester- day comes to Warrington with a good railway training. During the illness of Mr. Bishop, who died a short time ago, Mr. Williams had charge of the South Wales district and proved himself to be an energetic and reliable man. The district of which he is now the goods manager, extends from Warrington to Preston on the main line, and ineludei the Blackburn and Blackpool districts, and also the low level line from Broadheath to Runcorn.

Mr. Emmett, who for many years has been superin- tendent of the Waggon Department at Earlestown, has intimated his intention of retiring from the company's service at the end of June, and the vacancy thus created is to be filled by Mr. H. D. E trl, the present manager of Crewe works.

THE LOT OF A LONDON TAILORESS. A Side-Light on an Important Subject.

The following letter, written by a London tailoress, is interesting, as showing the great boon which bile beans prove to the working classes :- 68, King Street, Regent Street, Gentlemen, Some time ago I was troubled with chronic constipa- tion and an acute pain in my aide, both being due to liverdisorder. Often the pain was so bad I could hardly lie in bed. A friend advised me to go to the Middlesex Hospital, and I did. After weeks of treatment, however, I could see no improvement, so I ceased to attend. Ism a tailoress, and as I have to work from eight in the morning to eight at night, you may guess what misery I endured. Seeing in the paper an account of a case like mine which had been cured by bile beans, I told a friend of mine that I thought of trying them. She had tried them, and said," Do! I'll buy you the first box!" I commenced with them,and they soon cured the constipation. It was not,however, until I W.lB through the second box that I felt what a wonderful change Bile Beans had made, I was regularly hungry • and enjoyed the heartiest meals without any ill-effects afterwards. Then the headache ceased, the bodily pains went also, and I found I was cured. Bile Beans have done me more good than I ever believed possible, _

Gratefully yours, M.ABEI. CLRGG

The reputation of the vegetable medicine so highly spoken of by Miss Clegg is built on a sure foundation, for bile beans, through their general excellence, have now become the reading family !medicine. Stocked by inedicins vendors throughout the world.

SALE OF HORSES AT WALTON HALL.

Big Prices Realized,

Buyers from various parts of the country were present at Walton Hall, on Friday, when the fourth unreserved sale of horses belonging to Sir Gilbert Greenell, Bart., was held. Mr. W. Bainbridge was the auctioneer. Prior to the sale a luncheon was held, about 300 guests sitting down. Before the company separated the toast of "The King" was honoured. Forty-six pedigree hackney har- ness geldings, mares, and ponies, and six shire horses were offered for sale and bidding was fairly brisk. The total amount realized was 4,827 guineas. Some of the animals fetched very high prices, the largest sum paid for a single animal being 230 guineas, a chesnut gelding, Pure Gold,being purchased by a London gentleman for that amount. A fine bay gelding, El Dorado, was bought by Sir Walter Gilbey for 210 guineas. The following is a complete list of the purchases : GELDINGS.

PROTECTING THE CHILDREN.

THE WORK OF THE N.S.P.C.C. IN WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT.

Interesting details of the useful work carried on by the National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children in the Warrington district were forthcoming git the annual meeting of the Warrington branch which was held in the Council Chamber at the Town Hall on Thursday last week. Mr. F. A. Frost presided over a large attendance which included Colonel Buckton, Councillor Bennett, Mr. J. I. Watts, Northwich, Mr. McGregor, Plr. W. D. Jeans, jun. (secretary), and many ladies. rvi

The SECRETARY read letters of apology for non- attendance from the Mayor, Mr. R. Pierpoint, M.P., Mr. Roger Parr, Col. Cornwall Legh, Mr. Sutton Timmis,lvir. T. Birohall, J.P., Mr. W. Long,, Mr. John White; Mrs. Bleckly, and said many others had been received. The Chairman said the letters numbered abbut sixty or seventy.

THE ANNUAL REPORT.

SUGGESTED NEW DISTRICT OR :ADDITIONAL

INSPECTOR.

The CWAIRMAN said they had met to review the genero work of the branch during the past year, which he thought they would find had on the whole been a highly satisfactory one. They hoped to bt) able to do still better in the future as there was plenty of room for improvement. _ _ _

, The -SECRETARY submitted the eleventh annual report of the committee which read as follows :—The com- mittee have to report a considerable decrease in the number of cases dealt with during the year ended 31st March, 1903. The total number of cases investigated in this period has been 303 affecting 962 children of whom 466 were boys and 496 were girls. In 246 cases warnings were given, 39 were prosecuted, 9 were other- wise dealt with, 8 were dropped, and 1 was on the 31st March pending. The prosecutions (39) which numbered one more than last year resulted in the conviction of 31 persons who were sent to prison for various terms aggregating 7 years an!: 10 months. Twelve other ppr- sons were censured add their cases adjourned to give them a chance of improvement, and two cases were , withdrawn owing to the serious illness of the offenders. 258 of the cases were neglect and starvation, 23 ill-treatment, 3 exposure, 5 ceasing to beg, 5 immoral surroundings, 2 abandonment, and 2 criminal assault.l Upwards of 1,000 supervision visits were paid during the year, and assistance in the shape of food, bedding, coal andclothing has in many cases been provided for families who were in immediate need. There are at present about 500 families in the district under the Society's observation: The diminution in the number of cases has been brought about partly by a continued improvement in the town ;of Warrington and partly by' an alteration which has been made in the district covered by the branch, the town of Widnes, which formerly pro- vided a great deal of the Inspector's work having been taken from this district and added to that of the Liverpool outer branch. This alteration took effect from the month of August 1902. It had become urgent to make some change in this way, as the work in the districts had become heavier than one Inspector could manage. It should be mentioned that the Inspector of the Liverpool outer district has in consequence of the change,begun to find his work beyond him, and there is now talk of either creating a new district to cover Widnes, Runcorn and Frodsham or of employing an ad- ditional Inspector either at Liverpool or here to assist in that neighbourhood. The committee are pleased to be able to report a successful financial year. The activity. of Mrs. Frost and the ladies who have assisted her has resulted in an increased income, and there is a credit balance of £4l 10s. The annual ball was algal, thanks to Mrs. Frost and the other ladies acting with her 'on the committee, unusually successful, the large sum of '£39 17s. 6d. be- ing realized thereby. Miss Broadbent continues to take an active interest in the branch of the Children's League of Pity, and the Society is indebted to her and the league for a most useful sum of money collected in that way. Inspector Hackett continues to give every satisfaction and the committee consider that the general improvement in the district is to a great extent the result of his energetic work. The police in every part of the district are also very good in assisting the Society where their assistance is needed. In War- rington especially the Chief Constable and the force generally have from time to time given most valu- able help. So also have many of the school attendance officers and schbol masters and mistresses. Thanks are also due to the Cheshire Needlework Guild (through the kind offices of Lady Brooke), and the several ladies for gifts of clothing, bedding etc., which are always ac- ceptable. The Inspector has always cases in hand where such things are very badly needed and will be grateful at all times to any ladies for worn-out cloth- ing, old bedding, blankets, etc." Colonel BITCKTON moved the adoption of the report. He said he thought everyone present knew the objects of the Society and the work taken in hand, and was very keenly desirous that it should be carried out as thoroughly as possible. The work of the Inspector had not been as heavy this year, as part of the district had been cut off, but the district that MS left seemed to have provided an enormous amount of work. They could not help feeling what a pity it was that so much of such work had to be done. Before the branch was started people seemed to think there would be nothing for it. to rin hot ;t ro‘,..a of work. The branch had been in operation for ten years and had done an immense amount of work in pro- tecting the children. Of course the work entailed financial responsibility, and the actual responsibility was undertaken by Mr. Waugh and the officials at head quarters, but still it was the duty of the branch to try and provide for its own expenses. He was pleased to say that the Warrington branch appeared to be doing very well.

Mr. MCGREGOR seconded, and said he thought the branch was moving on a little better than it was some time ago. The amount of work left to be done after their ten years missionary work in the neighbourhood was certainly very considerable. He thought, however, that the figures showed an improvement as the propor- tion of CASOS of cruelty seemed to be diminishing. ADDRESS BY MR. WATTS.

Mr. J. I. Wirrs, of Northwich, then addressed the meeting aud said he had the honour of being secretary of a neighbouring branch for some years and had recently become a member of the Executive Committee in Landon. He directed his hearer's attention to the origin of the Society. First of all, he said, Mr. Waugh tried ' to convince the nation that such a thing existed as neglect of children or what was commonly known as cruelty, and he had a very uphill fight to get the people to believe that this existed. Eventually he succeeded in ' convincing a number of his friends to suchamextent that he could hold a meeting at the Mansion House. At that meeting it was decided to form a society to be known as the London Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children. The work which the sociefy took in hand was (1) to collect facts to show the necessity for further protection of . children in the country • (2) to get the law altered in order to protect the children and (3) to enforce the law existing at that time. Mr. Watts showed how these objects were accomplished in spite of great difficulties, and in the year 1888 the first Cruelty to Children Act was passed. In the following year the London Society became transformed into a National Society. During the career of the London Society, a number of branches had been formed in the country to carry on the same work that was being done in the metropolis. These branches had been able to show that cruelty was not confined to the large towns or to the slums, but was just as ram- pmt id the small villages and. pretty country houses as it was in the darkest dens of London. He was proud to say that Northwich branch was one of those early branches as, he believed, was the Warrington branch. After the formation of the National Society, a number of provincial branches were founded each year. Mr. Watts referred to the provisions of the Act of 1888, which were (1) to give children the right to .proper treatment, food and cleanliness ; (2) to allow children to give evidence ; (3) to allow one parent to give evidence against the other; (4) to give magistrates the power to change the guardianship of a child who had been ill-treated and whose parents were unfit to be its custodians and (5) to give the right of entry into houses. The speaker showpd the great advantage which accrued from these pro- visions and, continuing, pointed out that the society had gone on steadily increasing the number of branches in the country. Gradually it had been shown that the movement was of national impoitance and in 1895 the , Society received the Charter from the late Queer( Victoria. The following duties then devolved upon it to discharge :—(1) to prevent the public and private wrongs of children and the corruption of their morals; (2) to take action for the enforcement of the laws for their protection; (3) to provide and maintain an organisation for the above objects; (4) to do all other such lawful things as are incidental or conducive to the attainment of the above objects. Mr. Watts proceeded to refer to the constitution of the Sobiety and spoke of the valuable work which could be done by ladies' committees. - elach branch had the power of nominating two members to represent it on the National Council which met in London once or twice a year. The National Council proceeded to elect the National Executive Committee to whoml was entrusted the management of the affairs , of the society. The work of each branch was to take ' charge of cases,collect funds,and distribute literature.ln his opinion those objects might be best attained by sub-dividing the branch into districts and seeing that ' each district had an efficient ladies' committee with an honorer-- secretary and correspondent. The secret of success, in obtaining a maximum amount of funds for a branch lay in appointing a lady representative in every nook and corner of the district. He advocated

meetings of the district ladies' committee at which information as to the work that was being done by the branch should be submitted. The mem- bers of the No rthw ch branch had also found that they maintained a maximum amount of enthusiasm by holding the annual meetings at various places in the district. In regard to the Warrington branch, the meeting could be held at Warrington one year, and at Frodsham or other places the next. This arrangement had the effect of obtaining a different audi- once at each annual meeting, and interesting more people in the work. (Applause). The report was then adopted. THE BALANCE SHEET. •

The SECRETARY read the balance sheet which showed that the receipts amounted to £309 6s. 11d., and the ex- penditure to £267 16s. 11d., leaving a balance in handof £4l 10s., which would go to assist the work of the society in other towns. Councillor BENNETT moved the adoption of the balance sheet which he said was highly encouraging. This was agreed to. ELECTION OF OFFICERS.

On the proposition of the CHAIRMAN, seconded by. Mr. AlcGaEGon, Sir Gilbert Greensll, Bart., was re- elected president.—The retiring vice-Presidents—Lady Greenall, Lady Brooke, and Mr. R. Pierpoint, M.P.— were re-elected with the addition of Mr. A. H. Lee who has previously rendered very valuable service as secretary of the brand'. The general committee was re-elected with the addition of Mr. Frost, Mr. Wild, Miss] Norris, Miss Tinsley, and Miss E. Bennett,and the Executive Committee was re-elected with the addition of .Miss Norris and Mr. Wild.

THE QUESTION OF COURT FEES.

Colonel MICKTON proposed a vote of thanks to Mr Watts for his address.

Mr. MCGREGOR seconded and asked if steps could not be taken to obtain a larger remission of court fees to the Society. The amount recovered during the year was only 14s. which he considered was very small. The SECRETARY said they were bound to pay the fees and it was only by the courtesy and kindness of the magistrates that remissions were obtained. Mr. WArrs said that owing to the efforts of the Society and the presenting of a memorial to the Chairman of Quarter sessions the court fees were remitted through- out Cheshire. _ _ _

A vote of thanks to the Chairman concluded the meeting.

FATAL FIRE AT WARRINGTON.

A CHILD BURNT TO DEATH.

FIREMAN'S SHOCKING DISCOVERY. ~,w -- ,

On Friday the fire bell summoned the firemen to an outbreak of fire at the stables of Mr. James P. Bell, contractor v.nd coal dealer, which are situated at Howley, immediately behind Percival-street. The fire had been noticed a few minutes before by a neighbour named Mrs. Taylor who gavethealarm. Pending the arrival of the brigade,Mr W. Lowe safely removed two horses from the stables. and a number of willing hands assisted to remove hens and produce from the burning buildings. Being entirely constructed of wood and tarred felting the buildings fell an easy prey to the flames. A fierce blaze cast a glare over the neighbourhood, and the backs of the adjoining houses were in danger of being ignited. For- tunately they escaped with nothing more serious than a few cracked windows. Superintendent and Deputy- Superintendent Turner immediately got the briglide at work, and directed their attention to saving those sheds which were not yet alight. A hay and straw shed con- taining a lam) quantity of fodder, and a loft where hay wechoppedb, together with a large hen-cote were en- tirely gutted. Among the debris 'were a waggonette, harness and some machines used for chaff cutting. The adjoining sheds were saved from destruction. Shortly before one o'clock. the firemen were about to leave the ruins when a fireman named Albert Black- shaw, in turning over some burnt straw, found the charred remains of Arthur Bell, a five-year old son of the owner of the burnt premises. The boy was burned almost beyond recognition and his remains were placed in a box and removed to the mortuary where they await a Coroner's inquiry. The sad event was freely discussed in the neighbourhood and sincere sympathy was expressed with the sorrowing family. Mr. Bell says that the deceased was in the habit of entering the shed to look for eggs and in the confusion he had not been missed. Mr. Bell left home at seven o'clock in the morning and did not return home until he heard that his premises were on fire. On arrival he found Mrs Bell in hysterics and unable to give any account of the fire. The deceased and his younger brother were last seenalive at 10 o'clock by Mrs. Hannah Read, wife of the licensee of the Howley Hotel.

===== 0002601_19030508_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
EVIDENCE AT THE INQUEST.

The circumstances attending the death •,t Arthur Bell, the five year old son of James Perciv 4 Bell, coal dealer and contractor, 8, Percival-street, NV,:i was burnt wc).. inquired into by Mr. S. Brig- house, county coroner,and a jury on Monday afternoon. The first witness was James P. Bell, father of the deceased, who stated that he last saw his son alive at seven o'clock on Friday morning, when he leit home to go to business. About eleven o'clock witness was told that his stables and strawshed were on fire. He hurried there and found that the place had almost been burnt to the ground. He afterwards heard that his son had been burnt to death in the stables, and had been removed to the police mortuary. He went there and identified him. The deceased had been for some tinae in the habit of going to the stables and straw shed searching for eggs. Nitness did not know the time the fire originated. His wife saw the lad at about ten o'clock in the yard; he did not appear to have been seen after that time. To his knowledge the lad was not in the habit of using matches, although he might have done so on Friday. There was an incubator in the place; it was working but it was some distance away. It was worked by paraffin oil. Some of the neighbours had said the fire might have been caused by the inoubator being pulled over, but that was not kiJ because he saw it standing afterwards.

Frank Turner, deputy superintendent of the Fire Brigade, gad that at a minute to eleven on Friday morning, he received information that a fire had broken out at the stables at the rear of Percival-street. He went there with die steam engine Major, and several members of the fire brigade. The stables and straw shed which were built of wood and covered with tarred felting were well alight, a portion having fallen in, but they succeeded in saving a portion of the stables and cartshed after two hours work. Just before leaving, fireman Albert Blackshaw was in the act of turning over some straw in the corner of the stable when he dis- covered the charred remains of the deceased. The re- mains were placed in a boy; and the police removed them to the mortuary. He had not the slightest idea as to how the fire originated. The first jet of water was played on the spot where deceased was found, otherwise the remains might not have been discovered. From the position of the truss of hay he was of the opinion that deceased tried to get behind to escape the flames. Mr. .Bell, recalled, said he was not insured against fire. This was a total loss to him, and he would not get any recompense from any company. A verdict of "Accidental death" was returned.

The Foreman (Mr. Whittle) said the jury required the Coroner to kindly express to Mr. Bell their deep sym- pathy with him. They all knew him as a hard-working man, and deeply sympathized with him in his sad loss. The Coroner (to Mr. Bell): That is not an empty expression of regret on the part of the jury. It is very hard for you indeed to lose your lad and your property, It is a sad set of circumstances, and the jury are saying what they really mean both with regard to you and your wife.
