Sequel to a Drowning Case at Latchford

Locks.

At the Manchester Assizes on Saturday last the case of Ashton v. The Manchester Ship Canal Company was in the list for hearing, but shortly before it was called on, Mr. Pirkford, K.C. (Mr. Woodruffe Fletcher with him), explained to Mr. Justice Walton, who pre- sided in the Civil Court, that this was a claim by a widow against the defendants for dajnages in conse- quence of the death of her husband; Thomas Ashton, on the 2nd of August last, which was caused by his. falling into the Lataliford Lock. Unfortunately there were 10 children left, and he and counsel for the plaintiff had been 9:inferring iogether with the result that terms had been arrived at with a view to the settlement of the action, but it nust be distinctly understockl that the company did not in anywise admit liability, and therefore that such se.tlemen.t must not in future be taken as any precedent if any future acci- dent should arise. He therefore asked permission to withdraw the record. Mr. Edward Acton (instructed by Messrs. Arthur Brown and Crompton, Solicitors, Warrington), said he was acting on behalf of the widow, and he consented to the terms as mentioned by Mr. Pickford, K.C. His Lordship thereupon allowed the record to be withdrawn. The terms of the settlement were not mentioned in court.

STREET-MAKING EXPENSES.

EXTENSION OF THE TIME FOR REPAYMENT.

A Boon to Property-Owners.

At the Warrington Town Council meeting on Tuesday evening Councillor James Evans moved, in accordance with notice given, that the following resolution of the Council of the 6th December, 1892, be rescinded: " That it be an instruction to the Finance Committee as and from March 25th next to declare the expenses incurred in the niaking of private streets, whenever a majority of owners of the properties abutting on such , streets may desire it, to be private improvement expenses, and to make such arrangements as may be necessary for allowing to the owners of the property referred to a period not exceeding three years for the payment of their apportionments of the cost of such private improvements." Councillor FORSHAW seconded and the resolution was carried Alderman Fairclough being the only dissentient. Councillor EvAxs:then moved "That the Finance and Estate Committee be empowered to allow owners of property within the Borough a period not exceeding seven years for the payment of expenses under the Private Street Works Act, 1892." He said he did not wish to make any comment on Mr. Forshaw's resolution, but it did seem a pity that they shouldhave two resolu- tions on the „same agenda "'paper aiming at the same object. He thought his resolution meant rather more than that of Mr. Forshaw. It was emphatically a resolution of the Council recommending to the Finance Committee a course to be taken. Whether it was emphatic enough to suit Mr. Forshaw he did not know. Mr. Forshaw had suggested that he should ask the Chairman of the Finalise Com- mittee whether he would be willing to submit such a resolution to the committee. He did not think the consent of the committee was needed, the word "em- powered" was sufficient, and he took it that the Finance Committee would not go in the face of the Council. The resolution he proposed would provide that the time for the repayment of street-making expenses would be extended from three to seven_ years. _ _

Councillor FORSHAW said he did not think the reso- lution went far enough. It was simply an authority or power to carry out something. The Finance Committee could please itself. He did not see why the Council should not have the right to definitely extendtheperiod of repayment from three to seven years. If the Council extended the time it was done, whereas if they merely empowered a committee the thing was not settled. The TowN CLERK: If the resolution is passed the extended time will be granted to any person who applies for it.

Councillor TINITION said some people might not re- quire the extension. They might wish to pay in less time but if they wanted the full seven years the com- mittee would grant it. He thought Councillor Evans' resolution was the better one.

Councillor PEMBERTON opposed the motion. It seemed as if the Finance Committee were to have the option. Councillor Tlis'NlON: It is not so. If a man applies for the extension there is no option. Councillor Pzm BRUTON : What objection can there be to Councillor Evans withdrawing in favour of Coun- cillor Forshaw?

The TOWN CLERK : A person would be empowered to pay nothing within the seven years if Councillor Forshaw's resolution were passed. He could wait until the. 7th year before paying a penny. _ _

Alderma' n SmEmbisr-asked witether the time would be extended for those people who, according to the agenda paper, were in arrears? Cries of "No, no." Alderman SMETHURST: lam glad of that. . _ _

• Councillor WILKINSON said he sawno reason for these motions. The cause of the trouble was that some people made no attempt to pay, and at the end of seven years the same thing would apply. If property-owners would come and sign an agreement to pay in yearly in- stalments the thing would be on business lines. Some of them did not recognise the debt they owed. Councillor BARTON said he had thought if they paid the interest every time a demand was made, that would be all right, but had been informed to the contrary. Councillor EVANS, in replying to the remarks made, said that his resolution enabled property-owners to make their own arrangements with the borough- treasurer or Finance Committee from time to time. Councillor Forshaw's resolution meant that they might or might not pay within es seven years. The resolution was then put and carried. _ _

Councillor Fonslism iniisted on putting his own motion to the vote, and the following was then pro- posed "That the time for the repayment by property. owners of private street works expenses be extended from three to seven years." He said his resolution would encourage property-owners to put up more pro- perty and bring more rates to the town. Counoillor PEMBERTON seconded and said he wanted the matter as clear as possible. He held no brief for people who would not pay, and it was unfair of Coun- cillor Wilkinson to suggest that the motion was brought forward on behalf of these people. Councillor WILKINSON said there were certain men who had made no endeavour to pay, and the Finance Committee determined that they should pay. He was quite willing to give an increase of time to the man who was willing to pay year by year. Councillor BENNETT expressed surprise at the Non- Traders advocating this, because it was a step in the direction of that municipal trading the London " Times " spoke of. They were setting up practically as a building society or a bank. They were financing the men who were going to build the houses, and he was surprised at the service of the agitation. Alderman SUTTON said they had passed one resolution and if they passed this they would have to go back, give notice, and rescindthe other. _ _

Councillor DODDS said the municipal trading the "Times "opposed was not one they had expressed any opinion upon. The municipal trading they objected to was that bAtween members of the Council and the Cor- po.vtion. He thought, 14 would be better to carry the second resolution as it was not optional. Councillor LORD said he thought COunwilivr For- shaw's resolution was the better because it was more definite.

A vote was then taken, and 7 voted for Councillor Forshaw's resolution, and 4 against. mu I The MAYOR declared the resolution carried.

The fact that both resolutions were passed, makes it obligatory on the Corporation to grant 7 years for the repayment of street-making expenses in all cases.

IRONWORKERS' MEETING. AMERICA'S PROGRESS IN THE

STEEL TRADE.

MR, COX AND HIS AMERICAN VISIT. Mr. Broadhurst, M.P., and Trades Unionism.

Local ironworkers assembled in goodly numbers at the Parr Hall, Warrington, on Saturday-afternoon when a meeting was held under the auspices of the Warring- ton Branch of the Amalgamated Association of Iron and Steel Workers. Tea was provided for about 400, the catering of Councillor James Evans giving every satisfaction.

The subsequent proceedings were presided over by Mr. Alderman Monks. He was accompanied on the platform by Mr. H. Broa‘lhurst, M.P. Mr. James Cox (general secretary of the Association), Mr. F. Monks, Mr. A. H. Crosfield, Mr. C. E. Parker, Mr. Councillor Tinnion. Mr. Councillor Thomas Burton, Mr. W. Burton (Wigan), Mr. J. Elwell (council member for Warrington district), Mr. A. Birch and Mr. J. Rudge. The CHAIRMAN said he would like to take that, the first opportunity, of thanking the Ironworkers' Associa- tion for the very proud position in which they had placed him,by asking him to occupy the chair. He felt particularly proud for two reasons. First because it showed the confidence that they reposed in him, having asked him as one of the " other side" to come forward and preside over what was practically a meeting of the ironworkers' union. The other reason was because the occasion seemed somewhat unique. The object of the meeting was to hear from Mr. Coxhis opinion uponwhat he saw when he was in the United States of America as one of the delegates of the Com- mission which was organised by. Mr. Mosely. The Commission in itself was a very remark- able thing; it was evidence that we in England were not as fast asleep or so dead as some people wished to make out. (Hear, hear.) He thought it showed:4 very great deal of cleverness on the part of Mr. Moselv that •he was able to suggest such a com- mission and it was also evidence of Mr., Mosely's liberality that he was able to organize it and pay the expenses of some 21 or 22 representative members of trades unionism in order that they might see for them- selves something of the conditions under which the trade of the United States was carried en. He had read a copy of the report of the Commission and he could assure them that it was most interesting and instructive reading. There were four or five reports concerned directly or indirectly with the iron and steel trade and he would suggest that these reports be specially printed and cir- culated amongst the members of the Union. (Hear, Bear.) No one could read those reports without feeling that the cost of the commission had been more than repaid by the careful statement of facts which the re- ports contained. He (Mr. Monks) would have the reports reprinted if the secretaries would undertake to see that each member of the Union had a copy. (Applause). He (the Chairman) had seen something of the iron and steel iron works in the United States and one thing that struck him very iforcibly, was the fact that in a great many cases,the managers were English- mem, Welshmen and Scotsmen taken from this country to maimee these large works. It showed that this country was not very tar behind after all, and that there were men in this country who,if opportunity was given them, would show the whole world how to carry on iron and steel works to the best advantage. What we suffered from in this country was that we had not the same opportunities. He was of opinion that if we in this country had had the same opportunities, the same natural advantage and the same favourable conditions which are obtained in the United States, instead of taking the second place as we seemed likely to do as regards iron and steel, we should have made greater progress than they seemed to have done. He was quite sure there was no finer workman in the world than the Englishman of to-day—(a.pplause)—and if the English- man was only given a fair chance he would go further and do better work than any other workman of any other country. (Hear, hear). What they had to do was to establish closer relations and more friendly relations between the workmen and the employers. (Hear, hear). If that could be brought about he wascertain the manu- facturers and workmen would have nothing to be afraid of and no fear of competition whether it came from America, Belgium or anywhere else. It was because he felt that it was incumbent upon everybody to try and bring about this more friendly ,feeling and closer relationship between employer and employed, that he accepted the invitation to preside, feeling that it was his duty to encourage in every way possible this friendly feeling, andhe hoped by so doing to help for- wardsuch a desirable end. Letters of apology for non-attendance were received from Mr. Walter Milner and the Rev. John Yonge. Mr Henry Roberts telegraphed to say that he deeply regretted that he was unavoidably prevented from attending the meeting. MR. COX AND THE MOSELY COMMISSION. AMERICA'S PROGRESS IN THE STEEL TRADE. Mr. JAMES Cox then addressed the meeting on the Mosely Commission's visit to America. At the out- Lset he said.that industrial progress in this country could only be made by complete unity between employer and .employed. We must secure absolute co-operation on, 'the part of labour and capital if we were to hold our I own and make any progress. One of the pleasures of his.life was to attend meetings of that character where ' they could come so elosely together. In the earlier days of their organization, Mr. Broadhurst was one of the zuost active helpers his (Mr. Cox's) predecessors had in building up the association and doing all he possibly could to benefit the workers connected with their trade. Referring to his visit to America Mr. Cox said., that many people who had visited the States had gone to afew of the very best works and had fallen into the unfortunate habit of comparing some of the best , American works with some of our worst. They had *come home with long faces and longer tales as to the ' lost supsemacy of the .iron and steel industory of , this country. He (Mr. Cox) determined that as far as 1 -possible he would not fall into that error. There was no denying the fact that so far as their trade was con- I oerned,Ameriea had made enormous headway of recent f years. It was no use shutting their eyes to that fact -and he would give them a few figures showing the enor- mous strides which had been made in steel making in 1 the United States. In 1870 only 3,031,000 tons of iron i ore were mined in the United States, while in 1901 the j total had increased to 28,887,000 tons. Of pig iron 30 years ago .the outpxt was little more than 1,500,000 tons, while in 1901 it had grown to 15,878,000 tons, and last i -year to nearly 18,060,000 tons. With regard to Besse- mer steel,-30 years ago America only produced 37,000 tons a year, but it had now grown to 9,603,000 tons. In open hearth steel whereas 30 years ago 1,339 tons were I produced, last year the amount produced was 5,687,000 I tons. Thirty years ago the total production of steel I ems 118.000 tons; last year it was 14,994,090. The ques- ' tien might well be asked "How is it that they have made such enormous strides ?" The answer was that they had to take into consideration the enormous development of the country itself and the .corresponding increase in home requirements, and also the enormous deposits of the raw material, and the enormous facilities which the Americans possessed to meet the requirements. With reference to the iron trade itself he believed the Ameri- cans had nothing to teach Its. He had been through many of the iron works and he confessed that if any employer of labonr in England were to call hisattention to pnddling work such as he had seen in the United States he (Mr. Cox) would at once say "Don't pay for , it:' (Applause.) Steam hammers were conspicuous by ' their absence in the American forges, and the old rotary squeezers which were abandoned in this country some gars oso were still in use. The iron trade in America

===== 0002601_19030508_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
The Examiner, Friday, May 8, 1903.

was a declining trade, as it was in this country, though' he had no sympathy with the people who said that the trade in this country wa.s entirely dead. He believed it would be many years before that would be the case. He believed we would want iron as long as there were puddlers to puddle it, and the better the iron,the longer the life of the trade would be. (Applause.) In regard to the steel trade in America, the state of affairs was very much different. There was absolutely no doubt ' that America had made enormous progress, and we had very much to learn. The progress, however' was not the result of the employment of better men; but of better methods and more up-to-date machinery. Mr. Cox described several of the processes in use in the American mills, and submitted figures showing. the enormous output of steel as compared with the quantity manufactured in this country. The speaker denied the statement that only young men were employed in the American mills. In one works he saw a puddler at work who was 72 years of age, and he ascertained that the ages of others were 68, 66. 60, and 58 years respectively; men who proved their merits were not discarded after they were 30 or 40 years of age. But a man must prove his grit and worth before he was 40; otherwise he must not stand in the way of younger men who had proved their ability. (Applause.) Mr. Cox pointed out that it would be use- less to copy the Americans in every respect,as in this country there was not the same demand for certain classes of work. If similar mills were erected in England some of them would simply become white elephants. Employers in this country might, however, copy methods which were in use insome of the American mills, and he was glad to know that there were employees in England who were prepared to run a risk by spending money to keep abreast of the industrial times, employers like the gentleman who occupied the chair that evening. (Applause.) The workmen should therefore, do their level best to co-operate with the employers who were prepared to run a risk. If they did so he believed there was no cause for fear as to the future of the trade in this country. He contended that the Americans were slow, considering their natural resources, their enormous quantities of coal and ore, which were easily obtainable. The men who had built up America were Britisher, and what Britishers had done in America could be done in England. In this country we must have a sympathetic attitude towards the workmen on the part of the employers, and on the part of the workmenthere must be a determination that their workshould be of the very best. So long as these conditions obtained he believed he would live to seethe day when, considering all the difficulties they had to contend with, the employers and workmen of England would hold the palm. (Applause.) SPEECH BY MR. BROADHURST, M.P.

Mr. BROA.DIIURST, M.P., who had an enthusiastic re- ception on rising to speak, said it was about 31 years since he attended his first meeting of the Iron and Steel Workers Union. The general secretary was then Mr. John Kane, a man of remarkable ability. (Hear, hear.) He was one of the best trades unionists of his day and did not limit his sympathies to his union or his services to his own society. His wife was one of the most in- tellectual and able womenhe (Mr. Broadhurst) had ever met. In those days trades unionism was not popu- lar, and he was one of those who went about appealing to the men to join the Union for the purpose of pro- moting their own interests. He believed that sound, ' honest, sensible andwell conducted trade organisations were as useful and as profitable to the employer as they were to the workman. (Applause.) After paying a tribute to the work of the late general secretary of the iron and steel workers association, Mr. tsroadhurat proceedect to refer to tho report which Mr. Cox had just presented. It was one of the best descriptive accounts of American industry he (Mr. Broadhurst) had ever heard. He believed it to be a reasonable and truthful report. He had come to this conclusion that after all there was something in this world beside money pleasure. He thought at any rate at his time of life it was perfectly safe for him to say that America with all its business, with all its magnificent rivers,the other side of which one could not see, with all the waterfalls that deafened one, with houses nearly touching the Imoon—(laughter)—he was quite satisfied to spend the reminder of his days in Old England. (Applause). The industrial lesson to be learnt was this, that, take the world through, and other conditions being equal, there were NO BETTER WORKERS IN THE WORE) TtIAI4 BRITISHER& (Applause.) This was the point—if they could bring together the employers and the workmen as they had been brought together that evening,Great Britain could defy the world so far as manufacturing was concerned. (Renewed applause). All depended upon meeting together face to face in a quiet sober room, and if they did so all the nonsense would be knocked out of them at once. He was of the firm conviction that the over- whelming majority of:the workers in this country— whether in iron, steel, stone, clay, wood, cotton, or any- thing else—were so reasonable and amenable to good fellowship and honest and fair treatment that there need be no strained relations between capital and labour if each side would recognize the other as having rights, and if they met before the fire broke out and not after. (Hear, hear.) That was the time when they could adjust their differences and weld together the forces which I otherwise could never be brought into touch. Why Icould not that be done? There was no reason in the world why it could not. The stronger the union the better the opportunity. They could not negotiate with a body which hadino leader and no organization. They i must have organization before they could negotiate; , therefore he had always declared that the Lest organized trades of the country and the strongest unions were the I best friends to commerce and to the employers. , Mr. Broadhurst facetiously referred to the Chairman ! voluntarily taking up a position between the trade union representatives of iron and stone. He thought, ! however, he would live to tell the tale. (Laughter.) What an object lesson? The Chairman's speech was not one got up for the occa.sion,but he (Mr. Broadhurst) believed that every word uttered by the Chairman came from his heart and that he meant everything he said. (Hear, hear.) Supposing the Chairman had been the owner, as it was called of the BETHESDA. SLATE QUARRIES he thr

There they had had three years of misery and of struggle for the bare common liberties and necessities of life, there theyhad witnessedpriva- tions if not starvation, agony, separation of families that would never probably be united again, and all j because one party to a great bargain refused reasonable I negotiations with the men or even to submit the differ- ence to the arbitration of the highest and best men in the land. It was a great lesson as to what could be done by each, recognising in the other common birth- , rights, and the manhood, friendship and respect for each other which ought always to prevail in joint undertakings. They did not complain about; it and never asked for compensation. Nothing could succeed in this world without. combined action and obedience and discipline on the part of the rank and file of the various associations. For that reason he wanted more workingmen in Parlia- ment. (Applause.) He was not suggesting anything but heasked all present who had heard the dispassion- ate, moderate, friendly, and sensible statement of Mr. Cox,whether he would not besanenormons acquisition to steady the wild forces of the House of Commons if they could get him within its walls. There ware plenty of places in the County of Durham that would be glad to take him, and he ttoped the members of that organization would sea- that Mr. Cox was soon elected. (Applause.) In that matter they would have to take action.

LIBERALS WHO DID NOT LIKE LA.ROUR MEN would have to like the:a, and labour man wno did not like Liberals would have to like them. (Hear, hear). They could not have without giving; he who took must also give. The speaker proceeded to allude to the question of taxation and said that at present, the smaller the income of the British subject, the heavier in proportion was the taxa- tion. Those who had the least,paid the most, audit' taxation were adjusted according to the ability to pay it—and his friend Mr. Crosfield had discussed the ques- tion many a time—every article of necessity which the workers consumed would be freed from taxation. (Ap- plause.) He had spoken on this question many times in the House of Commons and a year or two ago Sir IVlichTl Hicks Beach said that if his suggestion were carried out the workers would pay nothing. His reply to that was that the worker gave his life, his bone, his mind, his strength— all that he possessed—to his country in the art of pro- duction and in making wealth, and if he were exempt from taxation it would be no sin because of the services he rendered. He carefully avoided politics that evening but he wished them, whether Tory or Liberal, to think out these things for themselves in the interests of their own families and as a matter of justice to their class. They who neglected to take their proper share as citizens in the transaction of the business and the politics of the nation were unworthy of the rights of citizenship. and should be struck off the roll. (Applause.) He wanted them to be active. Ho did not say .which way they should vote or for what party, but they should read for themselves,as literature was cheap enough and the best newspapers of the land were within their reach for a Denny and many for a halfpenny. Thanks to the efforts of that wise, great and departed statesman, Mr. Glad- stone—(applause.)—they had every means at their dis- posal for reading; the public press of the country had been brought to the door of eVery man and woman. Let them, therefore, find out these things for them- selves. They were masters of the situation, and they must not grumble at what Parliament did. It wiLs they who built it up. They were tnepeoptv.--1.... co if Parliament. They were the creators of it, and f in their folly atd neglect they built up apower which imposed duties on them to which they were not justly entitled then they malt not find fault with those who were in Parliament, but they must find fault with their own neglect. In conclusion the speaker said he. hoped to see the day when Warrington would join Leicester, Woolwich, and all the other constituencies of the pro- gressive nature and help to create a Parliament and a power which should do a great work in relieving the oppressed of the land, and place a larger proportion of the burdens of the empire upon those whose financial shoulders were broader, and seethat justice was done to those who created, as against those who only used that which was created. (Applause). He congratulated them upon that gathering, and he herin-1 that as a result I there would not be a single ironworker in the district who was not a member of the Union. (Cheers). THE RESOLUTION.

Mr. A. BIRCH proposed the following resolution : " That this meeting of ironworkers does hereby note with much pleasure that large and increasing numbers of workmen are banding themselves together to promote their common interests. It earnestly trusts that all workmen 80 combined will exercise the vast potential power already in their hands for theamelioration of the entire conditions of industrial and social life. That trade unions are fatal to liberty, hostile to merit, and that their sole aim and purpose are to raise wages is a delusion based upon a fallacy. Believing that the good of one is the good of all, and that the good of all is the good of one, this meeting is of opinion that the estab lishment of organized bodies with whom employers can treat will diminish the number of strikes and facilitate arbitration. It views with satisfaction that those trades which have passed most peacefully through the changes of the past are those in which boards of conciliation and arbitration or sliding scales exist, and it views with apprehension the great industrial loss and appalling waste of Labour which is taking place where these methods are ignored. It again urges a fusion and amalgamation of interests between employer and em- ployed, and is of opinion that nothing will so readily obliterate the bitter memories of the past, allay the hostilities of the present and lessen the deplorable waste of labour foree in the future as the establishment of a more equitable distribution of profits in therealms of productive industry." He observed that their late leader, Mr. Edward Trow, Mr. William Whitwell and others were great forces in preserving industrial peace in the North of England. In the great transition which had taken place from iron to steel in the great indus- tries in the north, that tnansition had been made with I very little friction mid no strikes. He thought they in Warrington were dfawin,g into line. If they showed a bold front and both employer and employed did their level best, they would be able to more than maintain their own in the trade and commerce of the world. (Applause). Mr. JOHN BUDGE, in seconding the resolution, said he was glad to see both employers and employed repre- sented on the platform. The resolution was c irried unanimously.

PROTECTION FOR THEIR _FUNDS,

-..----- _ ! What had happened Since the British workingman had I neglected his own interest in national affairs? To-day I the funds of every trades union were at the mercy of 1 the law courts. Under certain given circumstances the i I accumulated savings of years n trades unions might be Ilost through the folly• or the illegal action of an official. The funds of trades unions were at the mercy of employers who could make out a cause of loss in i consequence of trades union action. In other wordsall that bad been gained after 50 years of agitation had , been taken from trades unionists by the judges of the land. (Hear, hear). It was almost lieartbreaking to some who worked so hard, and who gave so much in order to enable the workers of the country to protect tuemselves, to see how little it had been valued by the younger generation and to see the disastrous results 1 that had followed this neglect. Their best organized associations of workers were nothing in effect and power as compared with the organization of other unions.

VOTE OF THANKS TO THE SPEAKERS.'

The CHAIRMAN observed that they could not separate without expressing their indebtedness to Mr. Cox, Mr. Broadhurst and the vocalists for having afforded them so much pleasure. None of them could have listened to the speeches which had been delivered without having derived benefit from them in so3ne way. 1 Mr. F'. MONKS, who was cordially received, said he would not detain the audience as he knew many of them were in a dry and thirsty land. (Laughter.) He had Isimply to propose a vote of thanks to the gentlemen who had addressed the meeting. He hoped Mr. Cox would pay another visit to America because from the little conversation he had had with him he found he had not seen anything like one-half of the mineral re- sources America could show. If no other commission vs-aslsentithe hoped that at any rate Mr. Cox would be able to pay America a second visit and spend a little more time there. There was one district which would repay'any amount of trouble, arid that was Virginia. When he (the speaker) was there he met a nephew of General Lee, who said that they had re-discovered Virginia, and that they would not go back to the old slave-owning conditions if they had the opportunity. He (Mr. Monks) had listened with very great pleasure to the speeches which had been delivered. (Applause.) Mr. A. H.CROSFIELD who had a very hearty reception seconded the vote of thanks. He observed that when they had their last gathering in Thewlis-street school he ventured to characterize the speech delivered by their present Chairman as one that would make its mark in Warrington. TECHNICAL INSTITUTE CONCERT.

At the Drill Hall, Runcorn, on londay night, a high class concert was given in aift ot tne Day School sports. Unfortunately the weather was anything but propitious, and counter attractions also interfered considerably with the audience. Doubtless, however, had local music lovers known the excellencies of the concert there would have been a much stronger attendance. The lack of numbers which might have been worse, all things taken into account, VMS indeed the only, regrettable feature of the evening. The concert from the first note to the last maintaii a high artistic level, and there was nothing but that which made for a distinct musical success. The principal artistes were : Soprano, Miss Clara Collier (Liverpool Philharmonic) ; bass, Mr. William Sweet- man (London and Provincial Concerts) ; humorist, Mr. Reginald Mounttleld ; solo violin, Mr. Whitehead Liver- pool.) The Runcorn Orchestral Society, under the capable conductorship of Mr. Horace W. Handley contributed not a little to the charm of the evening by several choice selections of instrumental music which were played with considerable verve and expression the chief items being "Morning,noon,andnight in Vienna," "Faust," "Pirates of Penzance "and the "Honeymoon." Miss Clara Collier, the possessor of a flexible soprano voice, delighted the audience with her admirably selected'songs "Il Recto" and "Beloved it is morn" the former being, perhaps, her most successful effort. The rich bass voice of *Mr. Sweetnia,n was heard to ad- vantage in" So, Sir Page," "The Golden Vanity "and "Trankadillo." For the latter he was vociferously re- called and obliged by rendering in his best style the famous rollicking song entitled "Drinking." The choicest moments of the evening, from a musical point of view, were those occupied by Mr..l.R.Whithead. As a violin soloist he possesses considerable technical abilityand expressive power and his gifts were fully realized in "Legende" (Wieniawski) and " Valse Brilliante" (Alara). Mr. R. Mountfleld enlivened the proceedings with several humorous and musical sketches. and the audience was not slow to recognise his merits. On being recalled he rendered a humorous parody on "The honeysuckle and the bee." The entertainment was a complete success and the audience showed their delight repeatedly, in no half-hearted way. Mr. Mountfleld,the accompanist performed the duties which devolved upon him in a highly praiseworthy manner.

DEATH OF CAPTAIN ROWLAND,

RUNCORN.

We greatly regret to announce the death of Captain William Rowland, shipowner, Cunning Place, Liverpool, which took place at his residence Eversley, Moughland- lane, Runcorn, on Monday morning. A short time ago the deceased was operated on by Sir Mitchell Banks, for tumour, but Captain Rowlands gmd nal ly genii in spite of every effort. The deceasml gentleman was his ilwn pioneer to success. He commenced life in a humble way, and by sheer force of character won a high position in nautical circles. Originally commencing life on the boats he worked up a business which received a great impetus with the constrnction of the Ship Canal. In religion he was a Wesleyan and a Liberal in politics. Though in no sense a pi:bile men, the news or his death was a distinct shock to the townsfolk, who lament the sudden cutting off of se strenuous a life. The funeral took place on Thursday afternoon and was very largely attended.

The Mono-rail Scheme. We I:!arn, says the Financial Times " that steps are being taken to push on the construction of the Manchester mono-rail tine from Manchester to Liver- pool, the bill for which was passed in the last session of Parliament. A syndicate has been formed with a capital of £20,000 to exploit they patents of the mono- rail both in this country and abroad, and to promote a company to build the new line.

WAR%INGTON MUSICAL SOCIETY. The Organ Transferred to the Corporation. SUGGESTED RESERVE FUND BY MR. F. MONKS.

There was only a small attendance at the annual meet- ing of the Warrington Musical Society held on Monday evening in the Masonic Room Bold-street..

THE REPORTS.

The report of the hon. secretaries (Messrs. W. E. Atkin and F. J. Lord) showed the numerical strength n„f chorus and orchestra to be as follows :—Sopranos 58, being 10 more than in previous season, contraltos 40, being same as in previous season, tenors 24, being one more than in previous season, basses 34, being three less than in previous season. Total chorus 162, or eight more than in theprevions season. This gives a gain in 'numbers of 24 since the close 1900-1901 season. The Orchestra.—Number of members on the books_ (increased of course by professional help at concerts) remains at 33, as reported at the last general meeting. Referring Ito the subscription list,' the hon secretaries said:—The Inumber of subscribers to-the concerts is 142. as against 137 reported at end of last se 'son. The difficulty of still 'further increasing the list is, we venture to think, to be accounted for by the fact that each year there is more and more competition by reason of more anl more entertainments of one form and Another offered to the public, whilst also many of our supporters leaving town and going to live outside find it too far to come to the concerts, and their places are hard to fill. No effort is left unmade each year to gain new support, and it is somewhat disappointing to have to record a loss on the season. We are of opinion that the sale of tickets for the unreserved parts of the Hall may possibly be short of what it should be, in spite of the desire of the com- mittee to make the concerts what is commonly known as popular without detracting from their standard of merit musically. We should like to record our thanks to Mr. Pearson (leader of orchestra),and to Mr. Payton (organist and accompanist) for their loyal help and un-i tiring regularityin attendance at rehearsals; and at the same time desire to acknowledge our indebtedness to Mr. Crossley, Mr. Reynolds, Mr. Newcombe and Mr.. Johnson for their help. Also to the members of the committee we should like to express our thanks. The committee has not been called to meet often, but we think this only shows how well they had considered and planned their work, and it is, as always, most gratifying to be able to report an entire absence of the slighest sign of friction or disagreement at all in the work of the season just closed. The HON. TREASURER (Mr. L.W. Reynolds) presented the cash account for the season 1902-1903, from which it appeared that the total deficiency on the season amounted to £l6 14s. Gd. There was a balance over- drawn of £156 16s at the end of the previous year, but it was pointed out that this had been wiped out by the Corporation on condition that the Society's organ was transferred to the Corporation. The MAYOR remarked that Mr. Reynolds was never over-sanguine. If he were they would think they were in clover. There were two special items of increase, season tickets £6 155., and the other tickets .22 10s. There was another matter which might point tea better condition of things, namely, that in lieu of the trains, which were a costly luxury. the trains brought peoplei from a distance, and actually landed the people at the door of the hall. which was far more convenient than ' having to walk from the station. On the whole he was inclined to think that there position was better—at least he hoped so—than it was that time last year. If this progress was slow but sure, they would probably not have much reason to complain. .

STATEMENT OP MR. ATKIN IMPORTANT PROPOSALS.

Mr. ATKIN read lettei-a of apelogy for absence from Mr. .1. G. Critchley, Mr. Crossley. (who was on his way from Cannes), and Mr. .1. Arthur Edlesten (one of the ;Ace-presidents, who expressed the opinion that he should no longer remain in office as change of resi- dence did not permit him to attend the meetings rev larly.) Mr. Atkin proceeded to state that he wished to supplement his report by alluding to three important points. 'For four years the society and the trustees of the organ, for the benefit Of the Musical Society, had been in communication with the Corporaton to tryto arrange a more satisfactory basis. He was now happy to say that to all intents and purposes the matter was now closed, arid that the organ had been handed over to the Corporation on terms which the committee had approved of, and which should' be satisfactory to every member of the society. By that means the society had been able to wipe off the debt on the instrument, and the debt on the society up to 31st December, 1902, so that the figures in the balance sheet of .£156 Ms. 3d. overdrawn were not abso- lutely correct if the arrangement with the Corporation was completed. It was now merely a mattter of form, as the Parr Hall Committee had advised the General Purposes Committee to accept the arrangement. If so the Musical Society hadhanded over the organ which, although a valuable asset, had been ton certain extent a white elephant. They had actually only to account for a matter of .216 14s. Gd.— the debt which had arisen on the working of the past season. It was satisfactory on the one hand, and on the other not satisfactory. He admitted that the object of the sooiety was not to make money, but to pay its way and to afford to the town a certain amount of intel- ieutintra....cautatar.---rne soutcvy-ttaa-r,roy-wptir&c,ll,l.o..o two objects, but had failed to pay its way. It was pro- posed to give an opera in or about October. He was in sympathy with the idea, and hoped it would be a suc- cess. Thanks to Messrs. Bond, Newcowbe, Wallington, and Crossley, the sche ne had been brought to a head. Whilst their friends had been arguing for an opera, other friends had been thinking abotitithem, and for thre3 years from time to time h had had letters from Mr. Monks, one of their vice-presidents urging him to take certain steps, which he had not seen his way to do, namely, to provide a res frill for the society. About six weeks ago Mr. F. Monks asked Mr. CrossleY and the speaker to meethim to discuss the matter. Mr. Monks' idea was that the society ought to be a little more speculative in their engagement of professionals; that they ought to get bigger names and make their programmes more attractive. He (Mr. Atkin) repre- sented the difficulties and Mr. Monks saw that while the society was in debt it would be foolish to embark on a speculittionwhich might prove disastrous. Mr. Monks saw as a man of business that if the society had some- thing that theycould fall back upon to pay any loss then they might venture of a concert or a series of con- certs whichlmight be considered on a more speculative character than the society had attempted. Mr. Monks suggested the formation of-a reserve fund and he would read Mr. Monks' letter to the secretaries :—" I like the proposal to raise £5OO as a reserve fund for the society. It Would give the committee confidence in management and free everyone from the fear of a loss which they could not see there way to make up. This in itself would tend to improve the concerts and make them more successful. At any rate it could be tried, and even if it was all spent in mak;ng good losses incurred it would have been spent in the cultivation of music which everyone enjoys. As I don't know with what success such a proposal will meet, I will put my contri- bution to the fund as the last £5O, so that if the society raises £.150, I will make up the last „£5O, making the round £5OO. The interest in such a fund would form a substantial contribution to the society's :rinctime." He (Mr. Atkin) saw difficulties in raising the ' £5OO, but he was in sympathy with the idea which had been placed before the committee and met with their entire approval. Mr. NEWCOMBE suggested that some one not on the committee should move that the committee take the matter up. Mr. FIELDHOUSE moved that the committee con- sider what steps should be taken in the matter. Mr. DAKIN secondedand the motion Was agreed to.

The CFf A IRAI AN said he believed the Corpiiration in- tended to make Parr Hall available for theatrical per- formances - by enlavging the stage and makiir other alterations. He supposed • that would materially'facili- tate the production of an opera by the members of the society. -(Hear,hear.) Mr. Monks had made a very handsome and kind offer and it deserved the very best consideration that could be giKen to it. In Cathedral towns where it was usual to have a musical festival it was usual to appoint a certain number of stewards. These gentlemen guaranteed to the extent of £5 each and any loss on the festival was debited to them. This scheme relieved the musical society of any debt and at the same time it did not make a very large call on the stewards.

Mr. REYNOLDS said it would be a good thing if they could get a few of their friends who could afford it to give them guarantees.

RE-ELECTION OF MR. PARR AS PRESIDENT. On the motion of Mr. Cir.. Mum seconded by Mr. P. C. PRICE, Mr. Parr was re-elected president. The NfAvou said it was a great pleasure to him to be- long to the society, especially to have the honour of being its president. He was fond of music and of at- tending the society's concerts, where they were always sure of hearing good music well rendered because it was done spontaneously and gratuitously. (Applause). THE VICE-PRESIDENTS.

Mr. ATKIN stated that the society had lost one of their vice-presidents lately by the death of Mr. Robert Garnett. Unfortunately, for some years, Mr. Garnett had been unable to attend the concerts, but for a con- siderable number of years he had been one of the vice- presidents. The following gentlemen were elected vice-presidents: —Messrs. Ziba. Armitage, A. H. Crosfield, John Fair- clough, James Fairclough, R T. Fairclough.F. A. Frost, F. Monks, R. Pierpoint, M.P., Colonel Ridgway, H. Roberts, Roger C. Parr, and John White.

WARRINGTON'S MAY DAY.

A DELUGED PROCESSION.

There was nothing traditional about Warrington's 'celebration of the incoming of the " merrie Month." No charming old-time revels, no crowning of the May Queen found a place in the programme of events. Even the sun held sternly Aloof and rain fell in a merciless downpour to the great discomfort of the participants in the procession which cmstituted War- rington's May festival. Formerly the advent of May was allowed to pass in Warrington with scant recogni- tion, but two years ago a number of townsmen con- ceived the idea of holding an annual horse parade. The scheme was taken up with enthusiasm, the cordial support of owners and drivers of horses being speedily enlisted. Of late years horse parades have become the popular form of May celebration in busy manufacturing centres, and it is not surprising that in these days Of industrial activity such events as horse parades should supersede the quaint ceremonies of a byegone generation. The welcoming of May is not, however, the only object for which these parades are organized. The promoters seek to encourage the owners and drivers of horses to taken prid. in their equine charges, and the prizes awarded constitute un incentive to kindly and humane treatment. There could be no two opinions in the minds of those who witnessed Saturday's procession, as to the success which has been attained in this direction. The quality Of the horse-flesh was superb, and the animals were smartly groomed and generally in, excellent condi- tion.

Unfortunately the success.of the parade was marred by wretched atmospheric conditions. There were several heavy showers during the morning and the prospects for the remainder of the day looked gloomy. As in previous years, the horses and vehicles assembled on the plot of land behind the Blue Coat School, and judging commenced at noon. Sir Gilbert and Lady Greenall showed their interest in the event by attend- ing, and other olficials and members of the committee were present. The judges had no easy task in arriving at decisions, owing to the uniform excellence of the turnouts. The most valuable prize was a silver cup presented by Mr. Peter Davies, of Warbur- ton, for the best horse in the procession, and the adjudicator awarded it to Messrs. Peter Walker and Son (driver, Scriven). The same firm carried off the silver medal and a premium of Li given by the Shire Horse Society for the best pair (drivers, Whitfield and Darby), and the silver fpedal for the best horse in single harness (driver, Scriveu). Diplomas presented by the R.S.P.C.A. to drivers in each class whose horses bore signs of exceptionally good treat- ment were awarded to Me4srs. Thomas Whit- field, E. Gall, J. Gleave. W. Sudlow, Edward Smith, A. E. Ashton, E. Massey, W. Oxley,J. datton,C. Starkey,T. Buckley. J. Rafter, G. Donaldson and F. Hewitt. The list of prizes presented by the Parade Committee is given below. Lady Greenall distributed the special awards, and a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to her on the proposition of Mr. W. P. Rylands, who remarked that Lady Greene!l always took a keen interest in everything that was of interest to Warrington. He thought it showed a most remarkable degree of sympathy and interest in that movement for Lady Greenall to come there on a wet day. Her presence and smiles upon them went, a long way to make up for the absence of sunshine, but he hoped next year the sun would be present so as to leave nothing to want for their complete enjoyment and satisfaction. (Applause.) Sir Gilbert Greenall returned thanks and said it afforded Lady Greenall the greatest pleasure to come there and see that the horses were so well taken care of by the drivers.

Since noon the wea' ther had somewhat improved, but just, as the procession left the ground,rain fell in torrents and continued steadily for a considerable time. This mid the effect of diminishing the number of spectators, and also played great havoc with the gay trappings of the horses. The parade was practically a replica of last year's, the entries numbering almost exactly the same. I Very little originality was displayed in the decorationl of the.horses and vehicles, the unsettled state of thel weather no doubt having deterred manyowners from em barking on anything like a lavish display of orna- mentations. The procession was headed by the Borough Band and next in order came the Corporation Fire IBrigade with the two engines which had been tastefully ' decorated. The firemen with their brightly burnished iiethiota very Mart:- Messrs. -rover Walker anti Sons' turnouts followed. The big, powerful horses were in the pink of condition, and had been brightly adorned with the usual May Day apparel. A long line of turnouts followed, various sections being headed by the Pioneer, Messrs. Crosfield's, and 1 Walkers Brewery Rands. With a view to advertisement, ' many coinNotitprs displayed goods on the vehicles. 1 Messrs. !Crosfield's and the Wilderspool Fire Brig:ides 1 figured in the parade, and looked very spic and span; the members of the latter brigade wearing new brass , helmets. The route traversed was as follows :—Blue ICoat-street, Winwick-road, Tanners-lane, Froghall-lane, ICrosfleld-street, Sankey-street, Buttermarket-street, IChurch-street, round the lamp. Church-street, Mersey- ; I street, Bridge-street, Horsemarket-street, Winwick- -1 street, Pinners Brow, Orford-lane to the Fair Ground, 1 where the procession disbanded. Efforts were again made to swell the funds of local charities by means of a collection along the route; and in addition to a large ! band of collectors there were vehicles specially arranged ' for the reception of contributions. Owing to the miserable weather the number of spectators was not as large as usual, and the collection suffered in consequence, the amoant realized being 25 Last year £3O was. handed over to the institutions. ! The arrangements had been admirably carried out by a large body of officials, and special praise is due to the hon. secretaries for the valuable assistance they render- ed. The ()facials are:—President., Sir Gilbert &reenall; Vice-Presidents, Messrs. H. P. Densham, James Dodds, William Fletcher, C. Jack Son Holmes, William Long, ICollin Maciver, H. Milling, Alderman F. NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLESTOWN. [By A CORRESPONDENT]. The proposition to transform the pit in Wargrave- road, into an open-air swimming bath is sure to meet with the hearty support of all sections of the ratepayers. Such a healthy means of support is badly wanted in the district, and the provision of a swimming bath could be made at the place in question at very little outlay. This would obviate the necessity of having to use the canal, or the old Common pit, neither of which are conducive to health or decency, and it is to be hoped the members of the Council wih see the wisdom of Dr. Watkins' suggestion, and proceed with the carrying of it into effect without delay. The news of the release of Travers who was charged with the manslaughter of his wife was received in Earlestown with mingled feelings. Many people thought that Travers should have received punishment adequate to the offence, but with the more enlightened it was considered that the judge, from the evidence adduced, took the only wise course and that Travers received no more punishment than he deserved. There were extenuating circumstances, and no one will be more sorry for the act which brought him into the felon's dock, than Travers himself. The Committee having charge of the Golborne May Queen festival were well advised in postponing the event last Saturday in consequence of the inclement weather, but if the present outlook is any guide the weather on Saturday will be no more propitious. For the sake of the children however„it is to be hoped it will improve, in order that the festival may be held.
